3.  $\sum_{j <   i}\max_{a}|f^{(i)}(x^{(j)},a) - f^{\star}(x^{(j)},a)|^{2} <   \varepsilon^{2}.$

our main lower bound here shows that—with the same caveats as theorem 2.8—the scaling in (28) is near-optimal. $^{16}$

theorem 2.9. let a function class  $\mathcal{f}$  and  $f^{\star} \in \mathcal{f}$  with uniform gap  $\delta$  be given. let  $\varepsilon_t \in (0, \delta/4)$  be the largest solution to the equation

$$
\varepsilon_ {t} ^ {2} t \leq \underline {{\mathrm {e}}} _ {f ^ {*}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta / 2, \varepsilon_ {t}). \tag {29}
$$

then there exists a distribution  $\mathcal{d}$  such that for any algorithm with  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq 2^{-6} \frac{\underline{\epsilon}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}, \delta/2, \varepsilon_t)}{\underline{\epsilon}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}, \delta/2, \varepsilon_t)}$  on all instances realizable by  $\mathcal{f}$ , there exists an sequence  $\{x_t\}_{t=1}^t$  and instance with  $f^{\star}$  as the bayes reward function such that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] = \omega \left(\frac {\underline {{\mathrm {e}}} _ {f ^ {*}} ^ {\text {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \varepsilon_ {t}\right)}{\delta}\right). \tag {30}
$$

relating the eluder dimension to the disagreement coefficient. an immediate consequence of theorem 2.7 and (27) is that we always have  $\theta^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta ,\varepsilon)\leq \mathcal{o}(\mathfrak{e}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta)^2)$ . while this bound scales quadratically, we can show through a more direct argument that the value function disagreement coefficient grows at most linearly with the eluder dimension.

theorem 2.10 (value function eluder dimension bounds disagreement coefficient). for any uniform glivenko-cantelli class  $\mathcal{f}$  and  $f^{\star}:\mathcal{x}\times \mathcal{a}\to [0,1]$ ,

$$
\sup  _ {\mathcal {d}} \sup  _ {\varepsilon > 0} \theta_ {\mathcal {d}; f ^ {\star}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta , \varepsilon) \leq 4 \mathfrak {e} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta), \quad \forall \delta > 0. \tag {31}
$$

this result strongly suggests that the quadratic dependence on the value function star number in theorem 2.7 can be improved.

# 2.5.1 the policy eluder dimension

previous work which uses the eluder dimension to analyze algorithms for contextual bandits and reinforcement learning (russo and van roy, 2013; osband and van roy, 2014; ayoub et al., 2020; wang et al., 2020) only works with the value function-based formulation in definition 2.5. in light of our results for the disagreement coefficient and star number, we propose the following policy-based variant of the eluder dimension.

definition 2.7 (policy eluder dimension). for any policy  $\pi^{\star}$  and policy class  $\pi$ , let the policy eluder dimension  $\mathfrak{e}_{\pi^{\star}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)$  denote the largest number  $m$  such that there exist context-action pairs  $(x^{(1)}, a^{(1)}), \ldots, (x^{(m)}, a^{(m)})$  and policies  $\pi^{(1)}, \ldots, \pi^{(m)}$  such that for all  $i$ ,

$$
\pi^ {(i)} (x ^ {(i)}) = a ^ {(i)} \neq \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(i)}), \quad a n d \quad \pi^ {(i)} (x ^ {(j)}) = \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)}) \quad \forall j <   i: x ^ {(j)} \neq x ^ {(i)}
$$

of course, we immediately have that

$$
\mathfrak {s} _ {\pi^ {*}} ^ {\operatorname {p o l}} (\pi) \leq \mathfrak {e} _ {\pi^ {*}} ^ {\operatorname {p o l}} (\pi). \tag {32}
$$

we are not yet aware of any upper bounds based on the policy eluder dimension, but we can show that boundedness of this parameter is indeed necessary for logarithmic regret in the adversarial context setting (in a worst-case sense).

theorem 2.11. consider the adversarial context setting. let a policy class  $\pi$ ,  $\pi^{\star} \in \pi$ , and gap  $\delta \in (0,1/8)$  be given. then there exists a value function class  $\mathcal{f}$  such that:

1.  $\pi = \{\pi_f\mid f\in \mathcal{f}\}$ , and in particular some  $f^{\star}\in \mathcal{f}$  has  $\pi^{\star} = \pi_{f^{\star}}$  
2. each  $f \in \mathcal{f}$  has uniform gap  $\delta$ .  
3. for any algorithm with  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta t}{32\mathfrak{e}_{\pi^*}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)}$  for all instances realizable by  $\mathcal{f}$ , there exists a sequence  $\{x_t\}_{t=1}^t$  and instance with  $f^*$  as the bayes reward function such that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] = \omega \left(\frac {\mathrm {e} _ {\pi^ {*}} ^ {\text {p o l}} (\pi)}{\delta}\right). \tag {33}
$$

# 2.6 discussion

# 2.6.1 proof techniques

policy disagreement-based upper bound (theorem 2.1). our proof of theorem 2.1 builds on the regret analysis framework established in simchi-levi and xu (2020), which interprets igw as maintaining a distribution over policies in the universal policy space  $\mathcal{a}^{\mathcal{x}}$ , and shows that the induced distribution of policies is a solution to an implicit optimization problem which (when configured appropriately) provides a sufficient condition for minimax contextual bandit learning. following this framework, we also view adacb's sequential igw procedure as implicitly maintaining a sequence of distributions over policies, but with an additional key property: the support of the implicit distribution over policies is adaptively shrinking. this is enabled by adacb's elimination procedure and is essential to our instance-dependent analysis. we show that the implicit distribution over policies given by adacb is a solution to a novel data-driven implicit optimization problem (lemma c.6), which, when configured appropriately by adaptively selecting the learning rate with option i, provides a sufficient condition for optimal policy disagreement-based instance-dependent contextual

bandit learning. our proof introduces several new techniques to instance-dependent analysis of contextual bandits, including using disagreement-based indicators and disagreement probability to obtain faster policy convergence rates (lemmas c.8, c.10 and c.11). we also remark that the selection of the adaptive learning rate is non-trivial, and we derive the schedule option i by carefully balancing key quantities appearing in our analysis.

value function disagreement-based upper bound (theorem 2.3). the proof of theorem 2.3 consists of two steps. in the first step, we build on the minimax analysis framework of simchi-levi and xu (2020) and show that adacb with option ii always guarantees the minimax rate. a new trick that we use here is to carefully track the adaptive value of  $\lambda_{m}$  and use it to infer the exploration cost under the current instance. in the second step, we establish the  $\frac{\theta^{\mathrm{val}}\cdot a\log|\mathcal{f}|}{\delta}$ -type instance-dependent upper bound for regret. the analysis is driven by a key inequality (lemma c.21), which provides a sharp upper bound on  $\mathbb{e}_{\mathcal{d}}[w(x;\mathcal{f}_m)]$  in terms of the ratio  $\frac{\theta^{\mathrm{val}}}{\delta}$ . beyond giving a means to bound the (expected) instantaneous regret in terms of  $\theta^{\mathrm{val}}$  and  $\delta$ , this allows us to adaptively maintain an estimated lower bound for  $\frac{\theta^{\mathrm{val}}}{\delta}$  based on empirical data. we then use an induction argument to show that the specification of  $\gamma_{m}$  in option ii enables adacb to enjoy a near-optimal instance-dependent guarantee.

lower bounds. our lower bounds build on the work of raginsky and rakhlin (2011), which provides information-theoretic lower bounds for passive and active learning in terms of the disagreement coefficient. as in this work, we rely on a specialized application of the fano method using the reverse kl-divergence, but with some refinements to make the technique more suited for regret lower bounds. for theorem 2.2, we also incorporate improvements to the method suggested by hanneke (2014) to obtain the correct dependence on  $\log |\mathcal{f}|$ .

value function star number bounds disagreement coefficient (theorem 2.7). the proof of theorem 2.7 is somewhat different from the proof of the analogous policy-based result by hanneke and yang (2015). the key step toward proving theorem 2.7 is to prove an empirical analogue of the result that holds whenever  $\mathcal{d}$  is uniform over a finite sequence of examples. this result is given in lemma e.1, and is motivated by a property of the eluder dimension established in proposition 3 of russo and van roy (2013), with their “ $\sum_{j < i}$ ”-based definition changed to our “ $\sum_{j \neq i}$ ”-based definition. the proof of lemma e.1 trickier, however, as our “ $\sum_{j \neq i}$ ”-based definition breaks several combinatorial properties utilized in the proof of russo and van roy (2013). we address this challenge by proving a new combinatorial lemma (lemma e.3), which is fairly general and may be interesting on its own right. nevertheless, our upper bound is quadratic in  $\mathfrak{s}_{f}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}, \delta)$  rather than linear, and we hope that this dependence can be improved in future work.

# 2.6.2 related work

gap-dependent regret bounds for contextual bandits have not been systematically studied at the level of generality we consider here, and we are not aware of any prior lower bounds beyond the linear setting. most prior work has focused on structured function classes such as linear (dani et al., 2008; abbasi-yadtori et al., 2011; hao et al., 2019) and nonparametric lipschitz/hölder classes (rigollet and zeevi, 2010; perchet and rigollet, 2013; hu et al., 2020).

our work draws inspiration from krishnamurthy et al. (2017), who defined variants of the disagreement coefficient which depend on scale-sensitive properties of the class  $\mathcal{f}$  in the context of cost-sensitive multiclass active learning. compared to these results, the key difference is that our value function disagreement coefficient is defined in terms of the  $l_{2}$  ball for the class  $\mathcal{f}$  rather than the excess risk ball for the induced policy class. this change is critical to ensure that the value function disagreement coefficient is bounded by the value function star number, and in particular that it is always bounded for linear classes.

our work also builds on foster et al. (2018), who give instance-dependent guarantees for the generalized ucb algorithm and an action elimination variant for general function classes based on the cost-sensitive multiclass disagreement coefficients introduced in krishnamurthy et al. (2017). we improve upon this result on several fronts: 1) as mentioned above, our notion of value function disagreement coefficient is tighter,

and is always bounded by the value function star number and value function eluder dimension 2) we attain optimal dependence on the gap, 3) our algorithms are guaranteed to attain the minimax rate in the worst case, and 4) we complement these results with lower bounds.

lastly, we mention that while there are no prior lower bounds for contextual bandits based on the eluder dimension, wen and van roy (2017) give an eluder-based lower bound for reinforcement learning with deterministic transitions and known rewards. this result is closer in spirit to our disagreement-based lower bounds (theorems 2.2 and 2.4), is it applies to a carefully constructed function class rather than holding for all function classes, and mainly serves to demonstrate the worst-case tightness of a particular upper bound.

# 2.7 extensions

we conclude this section by presenting some basic extensions of our contextual bandit results, including extensions of our regret bounds to handle infinite classes and weaker noise conditions.

infinite function classes. as we have mentioned, algorithm 1, theorem 2.1 and theorem 2.3 trivially extend to infinite  $\mathcal{f}$ , with the dependence on  $\log |\mathcal{f}|$  in the algorithm's parameters and the regret bounds replaced by standard learning-theoretic complexity measures such as the pseudodimension, (localized) rademacher complexity, or metric entropy. this is because the analysis of adacb (see appendix c) does not rely on any complexity assumptions for  $\mathcal{f}$ , except for lemma c.1, which uses a standard uniform martingale concentration bound for the square loss to show that the empirical risk minimizer  $\widehat{f}_m$  has low excess risk at each epoch. therefore, to extend our results to infinite  $\mathcal{f}$ , one only needs to replace lemma c.1 with an analogous uniform martingale concentration inequality for infinite classes. such results have already been established in the literature, see, e.g., krishnamurthy et al. (2017) and foster et al. (2018).

alternative noise conditions. beyond uniform gap, adacb can also adapt to the tsybakov noise condition (mammen and tsybakov, 1999; tsybakov, 2004; audibert et al., 2007; rigollet and zeevi, 2010; hu et al., 2020), as the following proposition shows.

proposition 2.5 (regret under the tsybakov noise condition). suppose there exist constants  $\alpha, \beta \geq 0$  such that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(f ^ {\star} (x, \pi^ {\star} (x)) - \max  _ {a \neq \pi^ {\star} (x)} f ^ {\star} (x, a) \leq \gamma\right) \leq \beta \gamma^ {\alpha}, \forall \gamma \geq 0.
$$

then algorithm 1 with option i ensures that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (1) \cdot \min  _ {\varepsilon > 0} \max  \left\{\varepsilon t, \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\text {p o l}} (\pi , \varepsilon) a \log | \mathcal {f} |\right) ^ {\frac {1 + \alpha}{2 + \alpha}} t ^ {\frac {1}{2 + \alpha}} \right\} + \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (1).
$$

tightening the value function disagreement coefficient. the supremum over the action distribution  $p$  in the definition (14) of the value function disagreement coefficient is more pessimistic than what is actually required to analyze adacb. consider the following action distribution-dependent definition:

$$
\theta_ {\mathcal {d}, p; f ^ {\star}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f}, \delta_ {0}, \varepsilon_ {0}\right) = \sup  _ {\delta > \delta_ {0}, \varepsilon > \varepsilon_ {0}} \frac {\delta^ {2}}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta , \| f - f ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \leq \varepsilon\right). \tag {34}
$$

the regret bound in theorem 2.3 can be tightened to depend on  $\sup_{p\in \mathcal{p}}\theta_{\mathcal{d},p;f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon_t)$ , where  $\mathcal{p}$  is a set of action distributions with favorable properties that can lead to tighter bounds. in particular, the proof of theorem 2.3 implies that for any instance with uniform gap  $\delta$ , if  $\theta_{\mathcal{d},p;f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon_t)\leq \theta$  for all  $p$  such that  $p(\pi^{\star}(x)|x)\geq a^{-1}$  for all  $x$ , then adacb with option ii ensures that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (1) \cdot \min  \left\{\sqrt {a t \log | \mathcal {f} |}, \frac {\theta a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} \right\} + \mathcal {o} (1). y x
$$

the following result shows that this property leads to dimension-independent bounds for sparse linear function classes.

proposition 2.6. consider the function class  $\mathcal{f} = \left\{(x,a)\mapsto \langle w,\phi (x,a)\rangle \mid w\in \mathbb{r}^d,\| w\| _0\leq s\right\}$ , where  $\| \phi (x,a)\|_{\infty}\leq$  1. define  $\sigma^{\star} = \mathbb{e}_{\mathcal{d}}\big[\phi (x,\pi^{\star}(x))\phi (x,\pi^{\star}(x))^{\top}\big]$ , and let  $\lambda_{\mathrm{re}} = \inf_{w\neq 0,||w||_0\leq 2s}\langle w,\sigma^{\star}w\rangle /\| w\| _2^2$  be the restricted eigenvalue. then  $\forall \delta ,\varepsilon >0$

$$
\mathbf {\theta} _ {\mathcal {d}, p; f ^ {*}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta , \varepsilon) \leq 2 \alpha^ {- 1} \lambda_ {\mathrm {r e}} ^ {- 1} s
$$

for all  $p$  such that  $p(\pi^{\star}(x)|x)\geq \alpha$  for all  $x$ .

as a concrete example, if  $\phi(x, \pi^{\star}(x)) \sim \mathrm{unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})$  we have  $\lambda_{\mathrm{re}} = 1$ , so that the bound is indeed dimension-independent.

handling multiple optimal actions. for simplicity, we assume that  $\arg \max_{a\in \mathcal{a}}f^{\star}(x,a)$  is unique for all  $x$  in the main body of the paper. when such assumption does not hold, we keep the original definition of  $\pi^{\star}(x)$  (which makes  $\pi^{\star}(x)$  unique for each  $x\in \mathcal{x}$ ), while defining

$$
\pi_ {\mathrm {s e t}} ^ {\star} (x) := \left\{a \in \mathcal {a} \mid f ^ {\star} (x, a) = \max _ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} f ^ {\star} (x, a ^ {\prime}) \right\}, \forall x \in \mathcal {x}.
$$

we then make the following modifications to our framework. first, we modify the uniform gap condition (2) to require that for all  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ ,

$$
f ^ {\star} (x, \pi^ {\star} (x)) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) \geq \delta \quad \forall a \notin \pi_ {\mathrm {s e t}} ^ {\star} (x).
$$

second, we modify the definition of policy disagreement coefficient to

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\mathcal {d}, \pi^ {\star}} ^ {\mathrm {p o l}} (\pi , \varepsilon_ {0}) = \sup  _ {\varepsilon \geq \varepsilon_ {0}} \frac {\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} (x : \exists \pi \in \pi_ {\varepsilon} : \pi (x) \neq \pi^ {\star} (x))}{\varepsilon},
$$

where  $\pi_{\varepsilon} := \{\pi \in \pi : \mathbb{p}_{\mathcal{d}}(\pi(x) \notin \pi_{\mathrm{set}}^{\star}(x)) \leq \varepsilon\}$ . by doing so, all our guarantees for adacb extend to the general setting where  $\arg \max_{a \in \mathcal{a}} f^{\star}(x, a)$  may not be unique for some  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ .

# 3 reinforcement learning

we now give disagreement-based guarantees for reinforcement learning with function approximation in the block mdp setting (cf. section 1.2.1). before proceeding, let us introduce some additional notation.

additional notation. for any markov policy  $\pi(x)$ , let  $\mathbf{q}_h^\pi(x, a) = \mathbb{e}\left[\sum_{h' \geq h}^h r_{h'} \mid x_h = x, a_h = a\right]$  be the corresponding q-function. we likewise define  $\mathbf{v}_h^\pi(x) = \max_{a \in \mathcal{a}} \mathbf{q}^\pi(x, a)$ , as well as  $\mathbf{v}^\pi = \mathbb{e}_{x_1}[\mathbf{v}_1^\pi(x_1)]$  and  $\mathbf{v}^\star = \mathbb{e}_{x_1}[\mathbf{v}_1^\star(x_1)]$ . next, for any function  $v: \mathcal{x} \to \mathbb{r}$  we define the transition operator by

$$
\left[ p _ {h} ^ {\star} v \right] (x, a) = \mathbb {e} \left[ v \left(x _ {h + 1}\right) \mid x _ {h} = x, a _ {h} = a \right].
$$

we also define the bayes reward function as

$$
f ^ {*} (x, a) = \mathbb {e} \left[ r _ {h} \mid x _ {h} = x, a _ {h} = a \right]
$$

for each  $x \in \mathcal{x}_h$ . finally, let  $\mathcal{f} = \mathcal{f}_1 \times \mathcal{f}_2 \times \dots \times \mathcal{f}_h$  be the full regression function class, and define  $f_{\max} = \max_h |\mathcal{f}_h|$ .

# 3.1 the algorithm

our main reinforcement learning algorithm, regrl, is presented in algorithm 2. the algorithm follows the optimistic least-squares value iteration framework (jin et al., 2020; wang et al., 2019, 2020), with few key changes that allow us to prove guarantees based on a suitable notion of value function disagreement coefficient rather than stronger complexity measures such as the eluder dimension. the most interesting aspect of the algorithm is a feature we call the star hull upper confidence bound: compared to the classical ucb approach, which computes an optimistic  $q$ -function by taking largest predicted reward amongst all value functions in an

$l_{2}$  ball around an empirical risk minimizer, we add an additional step which first "lightly convexifies" this set. this step is based on techniques from the literature on aggregation in least squares (audibert, 2008; liang et al., 2015), and leads to more stable predictions.

in more detail, the algorithm proceeds in  $k$  iterations. in each iteration  $k$ , we compute an optimistic q-function  $\overline{\mathbf{q}}^{(k)}$  such that

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {q}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} (x, a) \geq \mathbf {q} _ {h} ^ {\star} (x, a) \quad \text {f o r a l l} x, a, h. \tag {35}
$$

we then take the greedy argmax policy defined by  $\pi^{(k)}(x) = \arg \max_{a\in \mathcal{a}}\overline{\mathbf{q}}_h^{(k)}(x,a)$  for  $x\in \mathcal{x}_h$  , and gather  $h$  trajectories as follows: for each  $h$  , we roll in to layer  $h$  with  $\pi^{(k)}$  , then choose actions uniformly at random for the rest of the episode. these trajectories are used to refine our value function estimates for subsequent iterations, with the  $h$  th trajectory used for estimation at layer  $h$  . choosing actions uniformly ensures that the data gathered from these trajectories is useful regardless of the action distribution in subsequent iterations.

let us now elaborate on the upper confidence bound computation. let iteration  $k$  and layer  $h$  be fixed, and suppose we have already computed  $\overline{\mathbf{q}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  and  $\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h+1}^{(k)}(x) \coloneqq \max_{a \in a} \overline{\mathbf{q}}_{h+1}^{(k)}(x, a)$ . the first step, following the usual optimistic lsvi schema, is to estimate a value function for layer  $h$  by regressing onto the empirical bellman backups from the next layer (line 5):

$$
\widehat {f} _ {h} ^ {(k)} = \arg \min  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {h}} \sum_ {j <   k} \left(f \left(x _ {h} ^ {(j, h)}, a _ {h} ^ {(j, h)}\right) - \left(r _ {h} ^ {(j, h)} + \overline {{\mathbf {v}}} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)} \left(x _ {h + 1} ^ {(j, h)}\right)\right)\right) ^ {2}; \tag {36}
$$

here the  $(j,h)$  superscript on  $(x_{h}^{(j,h)},a_{h}^{(j,h)},r_{h}^{(j,h)},x_{h + 1}^{(j,h)})$  indicates that the example was collected in the  $h$ th trajectory at iteration  $j$ . assumption 3 ensures that this regression problem is well-specified. let  $\mathcal{z}_h^{(k)} = \left\{(x_h^{(j,h)},a_h^{(j,h)})\right\}_{j < k}$ , and define

$$
\left\| f - f ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {\mathcal {z}} ^ {2} = \sum_ {(x, a) \in \mathcal {z}} \left(f (x, a) - f ^ {\prime} (x, a)\right) ^ {2}. \tag {37}
$$

at this point, the usual optimistic value function for layer  $h$  (cf. russo and van roy (2013); foster et al. (2018) for contextual bandits and jin et al. (2020); wang et al. (2019, 2020) for rl) is defined as

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {q}}} _ {h} (x, a) = \sup  \left\{f (x, a) \mid f \in \mathcal {f} _ {h}, \| f - \widehat {f} _ {h} ^ {(k)} \| _ {\mathcal {z} _ {h} ^ {(k)}} \leq \beta_ {h} \right\},
$$

where  $\beta_{h}$  is a confidence parameter. as observed in jin et al. (2020); wang et al. (2020), however, this ucb function can be unstable, leading to issues with generalization when we use it as a target for least squares at layer  $h - 1$ . our approach to address this problem is to expand the supremum above to include the star hull of  $\mathcal{f}_h$  centered at  $\widehat{f}_h^{(k)}$ . define the star hull of  $\mathcal{f}_h$  centered at  $f \in \mathcal{f}_h$  by

$$
\operatorname {s t a r} (\mathcal {f}, f) = \bigcup_ {f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f}} \operatorname {c o n v} \left(\left\{f ^ {\prime}, f \right\}\right) = \left\{t \left(f ^ {\prime} - f\right) + f \mid f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f}, t \in [ 0, 1 ] \right\}. \tag {38}
$$

we define the star hull upper confidence bound (line 6) by

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {q}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} (x, a) = \sup  \left\{f (x, a) \mid f \in \operatorname {s t a r} \left(\mathcal {f} _ {h}, \widehat {f} _ {h} ^ {(k)}\right), \| f - \widehat {f} _ {h} ^ {(k)} \| _ {\mathcal {z} _ {h} ^ {(k)}} \leq \beta_ {h} \right\}. \tag {39}
$$

when  $\mathcal{f}_h$  is convex this coincides with the usual upper confidence bound, but in the star hull operation convexifies  $\mathcal{f}_h$  along rays emanating from  $\widehat{f}_h^{(k)}$ . this small amount of convexification (note that  $\mathrm{star}(\mathcal{f}_h,f)$  is still non-convex if, e.g.,  $\mathcal{f}_h$  is a finite class), ensures that  $\overline{\mathbf{q}}_h^{(k)}(x,a)$  is lipschitz as a function of the confidence radius  $\beta_h$ , which stabilizes the predictions and facilitates a tight generalization analysis.

oracle efficiency. regrl is oracle-efficient, and can be implemented using an offline regression oracle as follows.

- at each iteration, the empirical risk minimizer in line 5 can be computed with a single oracle call.

- for any  $(x, a)$  pair, the star hull ucb function in line 6 can be computed by reduction to a regression oracle. in particular, to compute an  $\varepsilon$ -approximate ucb:

- for convex function classes,  $\mathcal{o}(\log (1 / \varepsilon))$  calls are required.  
- for general (in particular, finite) classes,  $\widetilde{\mathcal{o}} (\varepsilon^{-3})$  oracle calls are required. the key idea here is that we can reduce erm over the star hull to erm over the original class.

see section 4 for more details.

# algorithm 2 regrl

input: value function classes  $\mathcal{f}_1, \ldots, \mathcal{f}_h$ . number of iterations  $k$ .

# initialization:

- let  $\delta = 1 / kh$ . // failure probability.  
- let  $\beta_h^2 = 400h^2\log (f_{\mathrm{max}}hk\delta^{-1})$  // confidence radius.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {a n d} \beta_ {h} ^ {2} = \frac {1}{2} \beta_ {h + 1} ^ {2} + 6 0 ^ {4} h ^ {2} a ^ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {h + 1} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} _ {h + 1}, \beta_ {h + 1} k ^ {- 1 / 2}) ^ {2} \log^ {2} (h k e) \log (2 f _ {\max } h k \delta^ {- 1}) + 7 0 0 h ^ {2} s \log (2 e k) \\ f o r \quad 1 \leq h \leq h - 1. \\ \end{array}
$$

# algorithm:

1: for iteration  $k = 1, \dots, k$  do  
2: set  $\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h + 1}^{(k)}(x) = 0$  
3: define  $\mathcal{z}_h^{(k)} = \left\{(x_h^{(j,h)},a_h^{(j,h)})\right\}_{j < k}$ .  
4: for  $h = h, \ldots, 1$  do  
5: set  $\widehat{f}_h^{(k)} = \arg \min_{f\in \mathcal{f}_h}\sum_{j <   k}\bigl (f(x_{h}^{(j,h)},a_{h}^{(j,h)}) - \bigl (r_{h}^{(j,h)} + \overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h + 1}^{(k)}(x_{h + 1}^{(j,h)})\bigr)\bigr)^2.$

$$
/ / \text {c o m p u t e o p t i m i s t i c v a l u e f u n c t i o n v i a s t a r - h u l l u p p e r c o n f i d e n c e b o u n d}.
$$

6: define

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {q}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} (x, a) = \sup  \left\{f (x, a) \mid f \in \operatorname {s t a r} \left(\mathcal {f} _ {h}, \widehat {f} _ {h} ^ {(k)}\right), \| f - \widehat {f} _ {h} ^ {(k)} \| _ {\mathcal {z} _ {h} ^ {(k)}} \leq \beta_ {h} \right\}.
$$

7:  $\pi^{(k)}(x) := \arg \max_{a \in \mathcal{a}} \overline{\mathbf{q}}_h^{(k)}(x, a)$  for all  $x \in \mathcal{x}_h$ .  
8:  $\overline{\mathbf{v}}_h^{(k)}(x)\coloneqq \max_{a\in \mathcal{a}}\overline{\mathbf{q}}_h^{(k)}(x,a).$  
9: for  $h = 1,\dots ,h$  do  
10: gather trajectory  $(x_{1}^{(k,h)},a_{1}^{(k,h)},r_{1}^{(k,h)}),\ldots ,(x_{h}^{(k,h)},a_{h}^{(k,h)},r_{h}^{(k,h)})$  by rolling in with  $\pi^{(k)}$

$$
f o r \quad l a y e r s 1, \dots , h - 1 \text {a n d s e l e c t i n g a c t i o n s u n i f o r m l y f o r l a y e r s} h, \dots , h.
$$

11: return  $\pi^{(k)}$  for  $k\sim \mathrm{unif}([k])$

# 3.2 main result

we now state the main guarantee for regrl. our guarantee depends on the following "per-state" gap and worst-case gap:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta (s) = \min  _ {a} \inf  _ {x \in \operatorname {s u p p} (\psi (s))} \{\delta (x, a) \mid \delta (x, a) > 0 \}, \\ \delta_ {\mathrm {m i n}} = \min _ {s} \delta (s), \\ \end{array}
$$

where we recall that  $\delta (x,a)\coloneqq \mathbf{v}_h^{\star}(x) - \mathbf{q}_h^{\star}(x,a)$ . we adapt the value function disagreement coefficient to the block mdp setting as follows. let  $\pi_{\mathrm{unif}}$  be the policy that selects actions uniformly from  $\mathcal{a}$ . for each latent state  $s\in s_h$ , we define

$$
\theta_ {s} ^ {\text {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} _ {h}, \varepsilon_ {0}\right) = \sup  _ {f ^ {\star} \in \mathcal {f} _ {h}} \sup  _ {\varepsilon \geq \varepsilon_ {0}} \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \psi (s), a \sim \pi_ {\mathrm {u n i f}}} \sup  \left\{\left| f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) \right| ^ {2} \mid f \in \mathcal {f} _ {h}, \| f - f ^ {\star} \| _ {s} \leq \varepsilon \right\}, \tag {40}
$$

where  $\| f\| _s^2 \coloneqq \mathbb{e}_{x\sim \psi (s),a\sim \pi_{\mathrm{unif}}}[f^2 (x,a)]$ . this notion is closely related to the value function disagreement coefficient (14) for the contextual bandit setting via markov's inequality, with the context distribution  $\mathcal{d}$

replaced by the latent state's emission distribution  $\psi(s)$ . we additionally define the total disagreement for layer  $h$  by

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {h} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} _ {h}, \varepsilon) = \sum_ {s \in \mathcal {s} _ {h}} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {s} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} _ {h}, \varepsilon),
$$

and define  $\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\varepsilon) = \max_h\max_{s\in s_h}\pmb{\theta}_s^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}_h,\varepsilon)$ .

our main theorem bounding the error of regrl is as follows. as with our contextual bandit results, we focus on finite classes  $\mathcal{f}$  for simplicity, but the result trivially extends to general function classes.

theorem 3.1. algorithm 2 guarantees that

$$
\mathbf {v} ^ {\star} - \mathbb {e} [ \mathbf {v} ^ {\pi} ] = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} \left(\frac {\theta_ {\operatorname* {m a x}} ^ {\operatorname* {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} , \beta_ {h} k ^ {- 1 / 2}) ^ {3} \cdot h ^ {5} a ^ {3} s ^ {2} \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} k}\right),
$$

and does so using at most  $hk$  trajectories. more generally, the algorithm guarantees that

$$
\mathbf {v} ^ {\star} - \mathbb {e} [ \mathbf {v} ^ {\pi} ] = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} \left(c _ {\mathcal {m}} \cdot \frac {h ^ {2} a ^ {3} \max _ {h} \theta_ {h} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} , \beta_ {h} k ^ {- 1 / 2}) ^ {2} \log | \mathcal {f} | + h ^ {3} s a}{k}\right),
$$

where  $c_{\mathcal{m}} \coloneqq \sum_{h=1}^{h} \sum_{s \in s_h} \frac{\theta_s^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}_{h,\beta_h} k^{-1/2})}{\delta(s)}$ .

let us describe a few key features of this theorem and interpret the result.

- first, if  $\theta_{\mathrm{max}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\varepsilon)\propto \mathrm{polylog}(1 / \varepsilon)$  (e.g., for a linear function class), then—ignoring other parameters—we can attain an  $\varepsilon$ -optimal policy using  $\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{min}}\varepsilon}$  trajectories. this fast rate improves over the minimax optimal  $\varepsilon^{-2}$  rate, and is optimal even for bandits. this is the first fast rate result we are aware of for reinforcement learning in block mdps.  
- in light of the results in section 2 this implies that one can attain the fast  $\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{min}}\varepsilon}$  rate whenever the value function star number for  $\mathcal{f}$  is bounded.  
- more generally, if  $\theta_{\max}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}, \varepsilon) \propto \varepsilon^{-\rho}$  for  $\rho < 2/3$ , then  $(\delta \varepsilon)^{-\frac{2}{2 - 3\rho}}$  trajectories suffice for an  $\varepsilon$ -optimal policy. however, the guarantee becomes vacuous once  $\rho \geq 2/3$ . in other words, bounded disagreement coefficient is essentially for the algorithm to have low error, and it does not necessarily attain the minimax rate if this fails to hold. achieving a best-of-both-worlds guarantee similar to our results for contextual bandits is an interesting direction for future work.  
- to the best of our knowledge this is the first oracle-efficient algorithm that attains near-optimal statistical performance in terms of  $\varepsilon$  for block mdps. of course, this is only achieved in the low-noise regime where  $\delta_{\mathrm{min}} > 0$ , and when  $\theta_{\mathrm{max}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\varepsilon) \propto \mathrm{polylog}(1 / \varepsilon)$ .

we emphasize that while the dependence on all of the parameters in theorem 3.1 can almost certainly be improved, we hope this result will open the door for further disagreement-based algorithms and analysis techniques in reinforcement learning.

# 3.3 discussion

# 3.3.1 proof techniques

the proof of theorem 3.1 has two main components. the first part of the proof shows that with high probability, for all iterations  $k$  and layers  $h$ , the set  $\mathcal{f}_h^{(k)} \coloneqq \left\{f \in \mathcal{f}_h \mid \| f - \widehat{f}_h^{(k)} \|_{\mathcal{z}_h^{(k)}} \leq \beta_h \right\}$  contains the bellman backup  $\left[p_h^\star \overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h+1}^{(k)}\right](x, a) + f^\star(x, a)$  of the value function from the next layer, which ensures that  $\overline{\mathbf{q}}_h^{(k)}$  is optimistic in the sense of (35) and leads to exploration. then, in the second part, we prove a regret decomposition which shows that whenever the optimistic property holds, the suboptimality of  $\pi^{(k)}$  is controlled by the gap  $\delta$  and the value function disagreement coefficient  $\theta^{\mathrm{val}}$ .

the first part of the proof (appendix g) boils down to showing that the empirical risk minimizer in  $\widehat{f}_h^{(k)}$  in (36) has favorable concentration properties. this is highly non-trivial because the targets  $\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h + 1}^{(k)}$  in (36) depend

on the entire dataset, which breaks the independence assumptions required to apply standard generalization bounds for least squares. instead, following jin et al. (2020); wang et al. (2020), we opt for a uniform generalization bound which holds uniformly over all possible choices of  $\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$ . to do so, we must show that  $\overline{\mathbf{v}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  is approximated by a relatively low complexity function class, which we accomplish as follows. first, we show that—thanks to a certain lipschitz property granted by the star hull— $\overline{\mathbf{q}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  is well approximated by a function

$$
\widetilde {\mathbf {q}} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)} (x, a) := \sup  \big \{f (x, a) \mid f \in \operatorname {s t a r} (\mathcal {f} _ {h + 1}, \widehat {f} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)}), \| f - \widehat {f} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)} \| _ {\mathcal {l} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)}} \leq \widetilde {\beta} _ {h + 1} \big \},
$$

where  $\widetilde{\beta}_{h + 1}\approx \beta_{h + 1}$  , and where

$$
\| f \| _ {\mathcal {l} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)}} ^ {2} := \sum_ {j <   k} \mathbb {e} _ {x _ {h + 1} \sim \psi (s _ {h + 1} ^ {(j, h + 1)}), a \sim \pi_ {\mathrm {u n i f}}} [ f ^ {2} (x, a) ]
$$

is the latent state norm, which measures the expected squared error conditioned on the sequence of latent states  $\mathcal{l}_{h+1}^{(k)} \coloneqq (s^{(1,h+1)}, \ldots, s^{(k-1,h+1)})$  encountered in the trajectories gathered for layer  $h+1$ . this approximation argument is rather non-trivial, and involves a recursion across all layers that we manage using the disagreement coefficient. with this taken care of, the next step is to use the block mdp structure to argue that  $\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  has low complexity. to see this, observe that  $\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  is completely determined by the center  $\hat{f}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  and the latent state sequence above. since the latent state constraint does not depend on the ordering of the latent states, we can use a counting argument to show that there are at most  $|\mathcal{f}|k^{\mathcal{o}(s)}$  possible choices for  $\widetilde{\mathbf{q}}_{h+1}^{(k)}$  overall. this suffices to prove the desired concentration guarantee.

the second part of the proof (appendix f) proceeds as follows. define the bellman surplus as

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {e}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} (x, a) = \overline {{\mathbf {q}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} (x, a) - \big (f ^ {\star} (x, a) + [ p _ {h} ^ {\star} \overline {{\mathbf {v}}} _ {h + 1} ^ {(k)} ] (x, a) \big),
$$

which measures the width for our upper confidence bound. we use a "clipped" regret decomposition from simchowitz and jamieson (2019) to show that whenever the concentration event from the first part of the proof holds, the suboptimality of  $\pi^{(k)}$  is controlled by the confidence widths:

$$
\mathbf {v} ^ {\star} - \mathbf {v} ^ {\pi^ {(k)}} \lesssim \sum_ {h = 1} ^ {h} \sum_ {s \in \mathcal {s} _ {h}} \mathbb {p} _ {\pi^ {(k)}} (s _ {h} = s) \cdot \frac {\mathbb {e} _ {x _ {h} \sim \psi (s _ {h}) , a _ {h} \sim \pi_ {\mathrm {u n i f}}} \left[ \overline {{\mathbf {e}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} (x _ {h} , a _ {h}) ^ {2} \right]}{\delta (s)}.
$$

in particular, let  $n^{(k,h)}(s)$  denote the number of times the latent state  $s$  was encountered in the layer  $h$  trajectories prior to iteration  $k$ . our key observation is that bounded disagreement coefficient implies that for each state  $s$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {x _ {h} \sim \psi (s), a _ {h} \sim \pi_ {\mathrm {u n i f}}} \left[ \overline {{\mathbf {e}}} _ {h} ^ {(k)} \left(x _ {h}, a _ {h}\right) ^ {2} \right] \lesssim \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {s} ^ {\text {v a l}} \cdot \frac {\beta_ {h} ^ {2}}{n ^ {(k , h)} (s)}.
$$

in other words, the disagreement coefficient controls the rate at which the confidence width shrinks. moreover, since the width for latent state  $s$  is proportional to the number of times we have visited the state (even though the algorithm cannot observe this quantity), we can bound the overall suboptimality across all iterations using similar arguments to those employed in the tabular setting (azar et al., 2017; simchowitz and jamieson, 2019).

# 3.3.2 related work

our result is closely related to that of wang et al. (2020), who gave regret bounds for a variant of optimistic lsvi based on the eluder dimension of  $\mathcal{f}$ . compared to this result, we require the additional block mdp assumption and finite actions, but our bounds scale with the value function disagreement coefficient, which can be arbitrarily small compared to the eluder dimension (proposition 2.3). on the technical side, their algorithm stabilizes the upper confidence bounds using a sensitivity sampling procedure, whereas we address this issue using the star hull. ayoub et al. (2020) give similar eluder dimension-based guarantees for a model-based algorithm, though the notion of eluder dimension is somewhat stronger, and it is not clear whether this algorithm can be made oracle-efficient.

reinforcement learning with function approximation in block mdps has been the subject of extensive recent investigation (krishnamurthy et al., 2016; jiang et al., 2017; dann et al., 2018; du et al., 2019b,a; misra et al., 2019; feng et al., 2020; agarwal et al., 2020). in terms of assumptions, we require the rather strong optimistic completeness condition, but do not require any reachability conditions or any clusterability-type assumptions that facilitate the use of unsupervised learning. the main advantages of our results are 1) we require only a basic regression oracle for the value function class, and 2) we attain the optimal  $\varepsilon^{-1}$  fast rate in the presence of the gap and bounded disagreement coefficient.

we should also mention that the gap for  $\mathbf{q}^{\star}$  has been used in a number of recent results on reinforcement learning with function approximation (du et al., 2019b, 2020a,b), albeit for a somewhat different purpose. these results use the gap to prove that certain "non-optimistic" algorithms succeed, whereas we use it to beat the minimax rate.

lastly, we note that the value function disagreement coefficient is similar to the "low variance" parameter used in du et al. (2019b) to give guarantees for reinforcement learning with linear function approximation, but can be considerably smaller when applied to block mdps. for example, in the trivial case in which each emission distribution  $\psi(s)$  is a singleton, the value function disagreement coefficient is automatically bounded by 1, while the low variance assumption may not be satisfied unless the latent mdp is near-deterministic.

# 4 implementing the algorithms with regression oracles

in section 2.1 and section 3.1, we mentioned that both adacb (algorithm 1) and regrl (algorithm 2) can be efficiently implemented with an offline regression oracle. in this section, we provide more details on this implementation, and on the overall computational complexity of our algorithms. throughout this section, we deal with general (possibly infinite) function classes.

to start with, we introduce the regression oracle that we assume. let us first consider the contextual bandit setup where  $\mathcal{f}$  is the value function class. given  $\mathcal{f}$ , we assume a weighted least squares regression oracle, which is an offline optimization oracle capable of solving problems of the form

$$
\operatorname {o r a c l e} (\mathcal {h}) = \underset {f \in \mathcal {f}} {\arg \min } \sum_ {(w, x, a, y) \in \mathcal {h}} w (f (x, a) - y) ^ {2}, \tag {ro}
$$

where  $\mathcal{h}$  (the input to the oracle) is a set of examples  $(w,x,a,y)$ , where  $w\in \mathbb{r}_+$  specifies a weight,  $x\in \mathcal{x}$  specifies a context,  $a\in \mathcal{a}$  specifies an action, and  $y$  specifies a target. the above weighted least squares problem is very well-studied in optimization and supervised regression literature. in particular, it can be solved in closed form for many simple (e.g., linear) classes, and is amenable to gradient-based methods.

when one solves the regression problem (ro) using gradient-based methods like stochastic gradient descent (sgd), the convergence rate typically depends on the lipschitz constants of gradients, which further depend on the range of weights and targets. motivated by this fact, we further define a range parameter  $b$  which describes the range of weights and targets that our oracle accepts. formally, for all  $b > 0$ , we define

$$
\mathsf {o r a c l e} _ {b} (\mathcal {h}) = \underset {f \in \mathcal {f}} {\arg \min } \sum_ {(w, x, a, y) \in \mathcal {h}} w (f (x, a) - y) ^ {2}, \quad \forall \mathcal {h} \text {c o n s i s t i n g o f} \quad (w, x, a, y) \in [ 0, b ] \times \mathcal {x} \times \mathcal {a} \times [ - b, b ].
$$

clearly  $\mathsf{oracle}_b$  becomes a stronger as  $b$  increases. while access to  $\mathsf{oracle}_b$  is generally a very mild assumption even when  $b$  is large, in order to achieve better computational efficiency, in this section we will be precise about  $b$  and aim to invoke  $\mathsf{oracle}_b$  with  $b$  as small as possible.

in the block mdp setup, there are multiple value function classes  $\mathcal{f}_1,\dots ,\mathcal{f}_h$ . for this setting, we assume access to the regression oracle (ro) for each of the classes  $\mathcal{f}_1,\ldots ,\mathcal{f}_h$ . again, we use  $\mathsf{oracle}_b$  to denote our oracle, where  $b$  denotes that the oracle accepts  $[-b,b]$ -valued weights and targets.

# 4.1 implementation details

implementing adacb. we first show how to implement adacb with the regression oracle. there are three computational tasks in the algorithm which require us to invoke the oracle.

1. line 2 requires computing the empirical risk minimizer  $\hat{f}_m$ .  
2. line 8 and option i (line 4) require computing the candidate action set  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  for any given  $x$ .  
3. option ii (line 4) requires computing the confidence width  $w(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  for any given  $x$ .

the first task is exactly a least squares problem, and we directly solve it using oracle<sub>1</sub>. the second and third tasks are more complicated, and we need to design additional subroutines to reduce them to weighted least squares regression. lying at the heart of the reductions are two basic computational subroutines: confbound and confbounddiff, which we present in algorithm 3 and algorithm 4 respectively. specifically,

- confbound is designed to efficiently compute the upper confidence bound

$$
\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f}} \left\{f (x, a) \mid \sum_ {\left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right) \in \mathcal {h}} \left(f \left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) - y ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \leq \inf  _ {f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f}} \sum_ {\left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right) \in \mathcal {h}} \left(f ^ {\prime} \left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) - y ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} + \beta \right\} \tag {ucb}
$$

and the lower confidence bound

$$
\inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f}} \left\{f (x, a) \mid \sum_ {(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}) \in \mathcal {h}} (f (x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) - y ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} \leq \inf  _ {f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f}} \sum_ {(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}) \in \mathcal {h}} (f ^ {\prime} (x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}) - y ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} + \beta \right\} \quad (\text {l c b})
$$

for any given context-action pair  $(x,a)\in \mathcal{x}\times \mathcal{a}$ , based on a sample history  $\mathcal{h}$  and confidence radius  $\beta$ . it can be efficiently implemented with oracle $_{\beta/\alpha}$  given precision  $\alpha$ , as pointed out in krishnamurthy et al. (2017) and foster et al. (2018).

- confbounddiff is designed to efficiently compute the action difference lower confidence bound

$$
\inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f}} \left\{f (x, a _ {1}) - f (x, a _ {2}) \mid \sum_ {\left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right) \in h} \left(f \left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) - y ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \leq \inf  _ {f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f}} \sum_ {\left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right) \in h} \left(f ^ {\prime} \left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) - y ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} + \beta \right\}
$$

for any given context  $x \in \mathcal{x}$  and actions  $a_1, a_2 \in \mathcal{a}$ , based on a sample history  $\mathcal{h}$  and confidence radius  $\beta$ . it can be used to identify whether an action  $a_1$  is guaranteed to dominate another action  $a_2$  on a context  $x$ , and can be efficiently implemented with  $\mathrm{oracle}_{\beta/\alpha}$  given precision  $\alpha$ .

building on confbound and confbounddiff, we design a subroutine candidateset (see algorithm 5) that accomplishes the second task above, i.e., (approximately) computing  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  for any  $x$ . then, building further on candidateset and confbound, we design a subroutine confwidth (see algorithm 6) that accomplishes the third task above, i.e., computing  $w(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  for any  $x$ . therefore, by applying confbound, confbounddiff, candidateset and confwidth as subroutines, we can efficiently implement adacb using  $\mathrm{oracle}_{\beta/\alpha}$ .

# algorithm 3 confbound(type,  $x,a,\mathcal{h},\beta ,\alpha)$

input: type  $\in$  {high, low}, context  $x$ , action  $a$ , sample history  $\mathcal{h}$ , radius  $\beta > 1$ , precision  $\alpha > 0$ .  
algorithm: see algorithm 3 of foster et al. (2018) for convex function classes; see algorithm 2 and 3 of krishnamurthy et al. (2017) for non-convex function classes.

implementing regrl. we now show how to implement regrl with the regression oracle. there are two computational tasks in regrl which require us to invoke the oracle.

1. line 5 requires computing the empirical risk minimizer  $\widehat{f}_m$ .  
2. line 6 requires computing the star hull upper confidence bound  $\overline{\mathbf{q}}_h^{(k)}(x,a)$  for any given  $(x,a)$  pair.

algorithm 4 confbounddiff(x,  $a_1, a_2, \mathcal{h}, \beta, \alpha$ )

input: context  $x$ , actions  $a_1, a_2$ , sample history  $\mathcal{h}$ , radius  $\beta > 1$ , precision  $\alpha > 0$ .

algorithm:

if  $a_1 = a_2$  then return 0. otherwise, use the binary search procedure in foster et al. (2018) (if  $\mathcal{f}$  is convex) or krishnamurthy et al. (2017) (if  $\mathcal{f}$  is non-convex) to solve

$$
\begin{array}{l} \underset {f \in \mathcal {f}} {\text {m i n i m i z e}} \quad \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {1}) + \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {2}) - \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} \\ \text {s u c h} \quad \sum_ {\left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right) \in \mathcal {h}} \left(f \left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) - y ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \leq \inf  _ {f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f}} \sum_ {\left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right) \in \mathcal {h}} \left(f ^ {\prime} \left(x ^ {\prime}, a ^ {\prime}\right) - y ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} + \beta , \\ \end{array}
$$

and return the optimal objective value.

the first task is exactly a least squares problem, and we directly solve it using oracle<sub>1</sub>. the second task can be accomplished by the subroutine confbound (algorithm 3), as long as we can solve weighted least squares regression over the star hull of each  $\mathcal{f}_h$

$$
\underset {f \in \operatorname {s t a r} \left(\mathcal {f} _ {h}, \widehat {f}\right)} {\arg \min } \sum_ {(w, x, a, y) \in \mathcal {h}} w (f (x, a) - y) ^ {2} \quad (\text {s t a r - r o})
$$

for any  $\widehat{f} \in \mathcal{f}_h$  (recall that the star hull upper confidence bound is just a modification of (ucb) where we replace  $\mathcal{f}$  with  $\mathrm{star}(\mathcal{f},\widehat{f})$ ). luckily, as lemma 4.1 shows, we can always reduce (star-ro) by (ro).

lemma 4.1. suppose weights in (star-ro) are bounded by  $w$  and targets are bounded by  $b$ . then for any  $\alpha > 0$ , we can find an  $\alpha$ -approximate solution to (star-ro) using  $\mathcal{o}(bw / \alpha)$  calls to  $\mathsf{oracle}_{\mathcal{o}(bw / \alpha)}$  for (ro).

therefore, by applying confbound with the reduction above as a subroutine, regrl can be efficiently implemented with oracle $\beta/\alpha^2$ .

algorithm 5 candidateset(x, h, β, α)

input: context  $x$ , sample history  $\mathcal{h}$ , radius  $\beta > 1$ , precision  $\alpha > 0$ .

algorithm:

if  $\mathcal{f}$  is a product function class then

compute

$$
\mathcal {a} _ {\mathrm {c s}} = \left\{a \in \mathcal {a}: \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d} (\operatorname {h i g h}, x, a, \mathcal {h}, \beta , \alpha) \geq \max  _ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d} (\operatorname {l o w}, x, a ^ {\prime}, \mathcal {h}, \beta , \alpha) \right\}.
$$

else

compute  $\widetilde{a} = \arg \max_{a\in \mathcal{a}}\mathrm{confbound(high},x,a,\mathcal{h},\beta ,\alpha)$

compute

$$
\mathcal {a} _ {\mathrm {c s}} = \left\{a \in \mathcal {a}: \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d d i f} (x, \widetilde {a}, a, \mathcal {h}, \beta , \alpha) \leq 0 \right\}.
$$

return  $\mathcal{a}_{\mathrm{cs}}$

# 4.2 computational complexity

theoretical guarantees for the subroutines confbound, confbounddiff, candidateset and confwidth are deferred to appendix a. here we summarize the total computational complexity for our algorithms based on these reductions.

computational complexity of adacb. for adacb, we set the precision  $\alpha = \mathcal{o}(1 / t)$  for confbound, confbounddiff, candidateset and confwidth so that the error does not degrade the algorithm's instance-dependent performance beyond additive constants. in total, when  $\mathcal{f}$  is convex, adacb calls oracle $_{\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(t)}$  for

algorithm 6 confwidth(x, h, β, α)  
input: context  $x$  , sample history  $\mathcal{h}$  , radius  $\beta >1$  , precision  $\alpha >0$    
algorithm:   
compute   
 $w = \mathbb{i}\left\{\left|\mathrm{candidateset}(x,\mathcal{h},\beta ,\alpha)\right| > 1\right\} \max_{a\in \mathcal{a}}|\mathrm{confbound}(\mathrm{high},x,a,\mathcal{h},\beta ,\alpha) - \mathrm{confbound}(\mathrm{low},x,a,\mathcal{h},\beta ,\alpha)|.$    
return w.

$\widetilde{\mathcal{o}}(at)$  times over  $t$  rounds, and when  $\mathcal{f}$  is non-convex,  $\mathsf{adacb}$  calls  $\mathsf{oracle}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{o}}(t)}$  for  $\widetilde{\mathcal{o}}(at^3)$  times over  $t$  rounds. we remark that the total time spent by  $\mathsf{adacb}$  outside of these regression oracle calls is  $o(a)$  in each round.

computational complexity of regrl. for regrl, when the target accuracy is  $\varepsilon$ , we should set  $\alpha = c\varepsilon$  for a problem-dependent constant  $c$ . this requires that we solve  $\mathsf{oracle}_{\mathcal{o}(\beta_h / \varepsilon)}$  for the star hull, which reduces to  $\mathsf{oracle}_{\mathcal{o}(\beta_h / \varepsilon^2)}$  for the original class  $\mathcal{f}_h$ . altogether, we conclude that  $\mathrm{poly}(1 / \varepsilon)$  oracle calls suffice.

# 5 experiments

to evaluate the empirical performance of adacb, we replicated a simplified version of the large-scale contextual bandit evaluation setup of bietti et al. (2018), which compares a number of contextual bandit algorithms based on either cost-sensitive classification oracles or regression oracles on over 500 multiclass, multi-label, and cost-sensitive classification datasets. we found that adacb typically enjoys superior performance, especially on challenging datasets with many actions.

datasets. following bietti et al. (2018), we use a collection of 516 multiclass classification datasets from the openml.org platform. $^{19}$  this collection includes many standard datasets, including the uci datasets used in foster et al. (2018); see bietti et al. (2018) for details. beyond this collection, bietti et al. (2018) also used 5 multilabel datasets and 3 cost-sensitive datasets; we omit these for simplicity.

for each dataset, we simulate bandit feedback by withholding the true label. we work with losses rather than rewards, and provide a loss of 0 if the learner predicts the correct label, and 1 otherwise. we randomly shuffle the examples in each dataset, but use the same fixed shuffle across all algorithms.

baseline algorithms and oracle. we compare to a collection of baseline algorithms implemented in the vowpal wabbit (vw) online learning library.[20] for the oracle, we use the default online learner in vw, which fits a linear model (that is,  $\mathcal{f} = \{(x,a)\mapsto \langle w,\phi (x,a)\rangle \mid w\in \mathbb{r}^d\}$ ) with regression using an online gradient descent-type algorithm which incorporates adaptive (duchi et al., 2011), normalized (ross et al., 2013), and importance-weight aware (karampatziakis and langford, 2011) updates, and has a single tunable step size which is treated as a hyperparameter. this algorithm is an online regression oracle in the sense of foster and rakhlin (2020).

all of our baseline algorithms are based on classification or regression oracles. we use their implementations in vw, which incorporate modifications to allow them to run in an online fashion with the oracle above. for the classification-based algorithms, vw uses an additional reduction layer which reduces classification to regression. following bietti et al. (2018), we use the following algorithms.

- the standard  $\varepsilon$ -greedy exploration strategy (langford and zhang, 2008), as well as a purely greedy variant, greedy.

- bagging, also known as bootstrap thompson sampling (agarwal et al., 2014; eckles and kaptein, 2014; osband et al., 2016), which attempts to approximate the thompson sampling algorithm.  
- online cover, a heuristic version of the iltcb strategy of agarwal et al. (2014). iltcb itself provides an optimal and efficient reduction from stochastic contextual bandits to cost-sensitive classification, and represents the state of the art from that line of research.  
- regcb (russo and van roy, 2013; foster et al., 2018), an approximate version of the general function class ucb algorithm based on regression oracles. this algorithm was found to have the best overall performance in bietti et al. (2018), though it does not achieve the minimax rate for contextual bandits. we only evaluate the optimistic variant (regcb-opt), not the elimination-based variant (regcb-elim) as the former typically performs much better.

we refer to bietti et al. (2018) for more details on the algorithm configurations and hyperparameters.

beyond these algorithms, we also evaluate against squarecb (foster and rakhlin, 2020), which is the first optimal online regression oracle-based contextual bandit algorithm. squarecb applies the inverse gap weighting strategy in (7), but uses all actions rather than adaptively narrowing to a smaller candidate set as in adacb. our implementation applies igw at each step  $t$  with a learning rate  $\gamma_t$ . we set  $\gamma_t = \gamma_0 t^\rho$ , where  $\gamma_0 \in \{10, 50, 100, 400, 700, 10^3\}$  and  $\rho \in \{ .25, .5 \}$  are hyperparameters.

adacb implementation. we implemented a variant of adacb in vw, with a few practical simplifications.[21] first, rather than using an offline erm oracle (as in (ro)), we modify the algorithm to work with the online regression oracle provided in vw by following the strategy of foster and rakhlin (2020). the protocol for the oracle is as follows: at each round, we provide the context  $x_{t}$ , the oracle provides a predicted reward  $\hat{y}_{t}(a)$  for each action, then we select an action  $a_{t}$  and update the oracle with  $(x_{t}, a_{t}, r_{t}(a_{t}))$ . instead than applying igw to the empirical risk minimizer as in algorithm 1, we simply apply it to  $\hat{y}_{t}$ . this strategy is natural because the protocol above is exactly what is implemented by the base learner in vw, and thus allows us to take advantage of vw's fast online regression implementation.

for our second simplification, rather than computing  $\mathcal{a}_t(x;\mathcal{f}_t)$  using the reductions from section 4 (which also require an offline oracle), we use a sensitivity-based heuristic that takes advantage of the online oracle. this is described in section 7.1 of krishnamurthy et al. (2017), and is also used by the vw implementation of regcb.

finally, rather than using a data-dependent learning rate, we set  $\gamma_t = \gamma_0 t^\rho$ , where  $\gamma_0 \in \{10, 50, 100, 400, 700, 10^3\}$  and  $\rho \in \{.25, .5\}$  are hyperparameters (the same as for squarecb). we set the confidence radius as  $\beta_t^2 = c_0 \log(kt)$ , where  $c_0 \in \{10^{-1}, 10^{-2}, 10^{-3}\}$  is another hyperparameter.

<table><tr><td>↓ vs →</td><td>g</td><td>r</td><td>c</td><td>b</td><td>εg</td><td>s</td><td>a</td></tr><tr><td>greedy</td><td>-</td><td>-48</td><td>-51</td><td>-19</td><td>-6</td><td>-55</td><td>-64</td></tr><tr><td>regcb</td><td>48</td><td>-</td><td>6</td><td>31</td><td>40</td><td>5</td><td>-21</td></tr><tr><td>cover</td><td>51</td><td>-6</td><td>-</td><td>25</td><td>33</td><td>-8</td><td>-27</td></tr><tr><td>bagging</td><td>19</td><td>-31</td><td>-25</td><td>-</td><td>9</td><td>-33</td><td>-47</td></tr><tr><td>ε-greedy</td><td>6</td><td>-40</td><td>-33</td><td>-9</td><td>-</td><td>-45</td><td>-58</td></tr><tr><td>squarecb</td><td>55</td><td>-5</td><td>8</td><td>33</td><td>45</td><td>-</td><td>-23</td></tr><tr><td>adacb</td><td>64</td><td>21</td><td>27</td><td>47</td><td>58</td><td>23</td><td>-</td></tr></table>

table 1: head-to-head win/loss rates for datasets with  $a \geq 3$  actions. each (row, column) entry indicates the statistically significant win-loss difference between the row algorithm and the column algorithm.

evaluation. we evaluate the performance of each algorithm on a given dataset using the progressive validation (pv) loss (blum et al., 1999). for each algorithm above, we choose the best hyperparameter configuration for a given dataset based on the pv loss.

<table><tr><td>↓ vs →</td><td>g</td><td>r</td><td>c</td><td>b</td><td>εg</td><td>s</td><td>a</td></tr><tr><td>greedy</td><td>-</td><td>-122</td><td>-127</td><td>-50</td><td>-4</td><td>-86</td><td>-114</td></tr><tr><td>regcb</td><td>122</td><td>-</td><td>1</td><td>81</td><td>122</td><td>71</td><td>20</td></tr><tr><td>cover</td><td>127</td><td>-1</td><td>-</td><td>63</td><td>122</td><td>52</td><td>3</td></tr><tr><td>bagging</td><td>50</td><td>-81</td><td>-63</td><td>-</td><td>63</td><td>-19</td><td>-63</td></tr><tr><td>εg</td><td>4</td><td>-122</td><td>-122</td><td>-63</td><td>-</td><td>-69</td><td>-111</td></tr><tr><td>squarecb</td><td>86</td><td>-71</td><td>-52</td><td>19</td><td>69</td><td>-</td><td>-44</td></tr><tr><td>adacb</td><td>114</td><td>-20</td><td>-3</td><td>63</td><td>111</td><td>44</td><td>-</td></tr></table>

table 2: head-to-head win/loss rates across all datasets.

to compare each pair of algorithms on a given dataset, we use the notion of a statistically significant win or loss defined in bietti et al. (2018), which is based on an approximate z-test. for each algorithm pair, we count the total number of significant wins / losses, using the best configuration for each dataset.

results: hard datasets. to evaluate performance on hard exploration problems, we restricted only to datasets with  $a \geq 3$  actions. head-to-head results are displayed in table 1. for this subset of datasets, we find that adacb has the best overall performance, and has a (large) positive win-loss difference against all of the baselines. this suggests that adacb may be a promising approach for solving challenging exploration problems.

results: all datasets. head-to-head results across all datasets are displayed in table 2. we find that for the full collection of datasets, regcb (foster et al., 2018) has the best overall performance, with a positive win-loss difference against every algorithm. adacb has strong performance overall, but narrowly loses to regcb and online cover, with a positive win-loss difference against all other algorithms. the strong performance of regcb mirrors the findings of bietti et al. (2018). they observed that many of the datasets in the openml collection are "easy" for exploration in the sense that 1) they have few actions (in fact, around 400 datasets have only 2 actions), and 2) even the simple greedy strategy performs well; regcb seems to be good at exploiting this. it would be interesting to understand whether we can make adacb eliminate actions even more aggressively for the easy problems on which regcb excels, and whether we can develop theory to support this.

# 6 discussion

we have developed efficient, instance-dependent algorithms for contextual bandits and reinforcement learning with function approximation. we showed that disagreement coefficients and related combinatorial parameters play a fundamental role in determining the optimal instance-dependent rates, and that algorithms that adapt to these parameters can be simple and practically effective. our results suggest many fruitful directions for future research.

for contextual bandits, there are a number of very interesting and practically relevant questions:

- for adversarial contexts, can we develop instance-dependent algorithms that are efficient in terms of online regression oracles, as in foster and rakhlin (2020)?  
- can we reduce the total number of regression oracle calls used by adacb to  $\tilde{o}(\mathrm{polylog}(t))$ , thereby matching the runtime of simchi-levi and xu (2020) for non-instance-dependent regret?  
- can we extend our algorithms and complexity measures to optimally handle infinite actions?

beyond these questions, we hope to see the various gaps between our upper and lower bounds closed.

for reinforcement learning, we are excited to see whether our analysis techniques can be applied more broadly, and to develop more refined lower bounds that better reflect the role of disagreement in determining the difficulty of exploration. on the technical side, there are many possible improvements to theorem 3.1. for

example, is it possible to develop best-of-both-worlds guarantees similar to those attained by adacb, or even to efficiently attain the minimax rate in the absence of bounded gap and disagreement coefficient? can we improve the dependence on  $s$ ,  $a$ , and so forth to match the optimal rates for the tabular setting?

# organization of appendix

this appendix is organized as follows. appendix a contains details for the computational results presented in section 4. part i contains proofs for our contextual bandit results, and part ii contain proofs for our reinforcement learning results.

# a computational guarantees

in what follows, we establish computational guarantees for the subroutines confbound, confbounddiff, candidateset and confwidth.

computation of confidence bounds. we first provide guarantees for confbound and confbounddiff, in lemma a.1 and lemma a.2 respectively. lemma a.1 is adapted from known results in the literature. the proof of lemma a.2 can be found in appendix a.1.

lemma a.1 (theorem 1 in foster et al. 2018 & theorem 1 in krishnamurthy et al. 2017). consider the adacb setting. let  $\mathcal{h}_m = \{(x_t, a_t, r_t(a_t))\}_{t=1}^{t_{m-1}}$ . if the function class  $\mathcal{f}$  is convex and closed under pointwise convergence, then for any  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ , the computation procedures

$$
\operatorname {c o n f b o u n d} (\operatorname {h i g h}, x, a, \mathcal {h} _ {m}, \beta_ {m}, \alpha) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d} (\operatorname {l o w}, x, a, \mathcal {h} _ {m}, \beta_ {m}, \alpha)
$$

terminate after  $\mathcal{o}(\log (1 / \alpha))$  calls to oracle<sub>1</sub>, and the returned values satisfy

$$
\left| \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} f (x, a) - \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d} (\operatorname {h i g h}, x, a, \mathcal {h} _ {m}, \beta_ {m}, \alpha) \right| \leq \alpha ,
$$

$$
\left| \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} f (x, a) - \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d} (\operatorname {l o w}, x, a, \mathcal {h} _ {m}, \beta_ {m}, \alpha) \right| \leq \alpha .
$$

if the function class  $\mathcal{f}$  is non-convex, then the required number of oracle calls is  $\mathcal{o}(1 / \alpha^2\log (1 / \alpha))$

lemma a.2. consider the adacb setting. let  $\mathcal{h}_m = \{(x_t, a_t, r_t(a_t))\}_{t=1}^{t_{m-1}}$ . if the function class  $\mathcal{f}$  is convex and closed under pointwise convergence, then for any  $x \in \mathcal{x}$  and  $a_1, a_2 \in \mathcal{a}$ , the computation procedure

$$
\operatorname {c o n f b o u n d d i f f} \left(x, a _ {1}, a _ {2}, \mathcal {h} _ {m}, \beta_ {m}, \alpha\right)
$$

terminates after  $\mathcal{o}(\log (1 / \alpha))$  calls to  $\mathrm{oracle}_{1 / \alpha}$ , and the returned values satisfy

$$
\left| \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \left(f (x, a _ {1}) - f (x, a _ {2})\right) - \operatorname {c o n f b o u n d d i f f} \left(x, a _ {1}, a _ {2}, \mathcal {h} _ {m}, \beta_ {m}, \alpha\right) \right| \leq 2 \alpha .
$$

if the function class  $\mathcal{f}$  is non-convex, then the required number of oracle calls is  $\mathcal{o}(1 / \alpha^2\log (1 / \alpha))$

lemma a.1 and lemma a.2 show that confbound and confbounddiff compute the desired confidence bounds up to a precision of  $\alpha$  in  $\mathcal{o}(\log(1/\alpha))$  or  $\mathcal{o}(1/\alpha^2\log(1/\alpha))$  iterations. note that both lemma a.1 and lemma a.2 are stated in the adacb setting. for the sake of brevity, we omit the guarantee of confbound for the regrl setting here, as it is essentially the same as lemma a.1, with only notations changing.

computation of candidate action sets and confidence width. we now provide guarantees for confbounddiff and confwidth. all the results in this part are stated in the adacb setting, as candidateset and confwidth are only required for adacb.

we discuss two cases: when  $\mathcal{f}$  is a product function class, it is possible for candidateset to precisely compute  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$ ; when  $\mathcal{f}$  is not a product function class, precisely computing  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  becomes difficult, yet it is possible for candidateset to precisely determine whether  $|\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)| > 1$ , which is already sufficient for confwidth to precisely compute  $w(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  and for adacb to achieve all the statistical guarantees stated in section 2.

case 1: product function class. when  $\mathcal{f}$  is a product function class, i.e.  $\mathcal{f} = \mathcal{g}^{\mathcal{a}}$  for some function class  $\mathcal{g}$ , it is possible for adacb to maintain each version space  $\mathcal{f}_m$  as a product function class, which makes it especially simple to compute  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$ . to ensure the product structure of  $\mathcal{f}_m$ , we need to make slight modifications to the definition of  $\mathcal{f}_m$  in line 3 of adacb.

definition a.1 (alternative definition of  $\mathcal{f}_m$  for adacb). if  $\mathcal{f}$  is a product function class, i.e.,  $\mathcal{f} = \mathcal{g}^{\mathcal{a}}$  for some  $\mathcal{g}$ , then at line 3 of algorithm 1, we define  $\mathcal{f}_m = \prod_{a\in \mathcal{a}}\mathcal{g}_{m,a}$ , where

$$
\mathcal {g} _ {m, a} = \left\{g \in \mathcal {g} \big | \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t _ {m - 1}} (g (x _ {t}) - r _ {t} (a _ {t})) ^ {2} \mathbb {i} \{a _ {t} = a \} \leq \inf  _ {g ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {g}} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t _ {m - 1}} (g ^ {\prime} (x _ {t}) - r _ {t} (a _ {t})) ^ {2} \mathbb {i} \{a _ {t} = a \} + \beta_ {m} \right\}.
$$

it is not difficult to see that the above modifications do not affect adacb's statistical guarantees in section 2,[22] or confbound's computational guarantee in lemma a.1. the following lemma demonstrates the value of the product structure of  $\mathcal{f}_m$ .

lemma a.3. if  $\mathcal{f}' \subset \mathcal{f}$  is a product function class, then for any  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ ,

$$
\mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}) = \left\{a \in \mathcal {a}: \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}} f (x, a) \geq \max  _ {a ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {a}} \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}} f (x, a ^ {\prime}) \right\},
$$

$$
w (x; \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}) = \mathbb {i} \left\{\left| \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}) \right| > 1 \right\} \max  _ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \left| \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}} f (x, a) - \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}} f (x, a) \right|.
$$

lemma a.3 implies that if  $\mathcal{f}$  is a product function class (and thus  $\mathcal{f}_m$  is a product function class under definition a.1), then both  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  and  $w(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  can be explicitly expressed in terms of the upper and lower confidence bounds  $\sup_{f\in \mathcal{f}_m}f(x,a)$  and  $\inf_{f\in \mathcal{f}_m}f(x,a)$ . since confbound enables us to compute  $\sup_{f\in \mathcal{f}_m}f(x,a)$  and  $\inf_{f\in \mathcal{f}_m}f(x,a)$  for all  $a\in \mathcal{a}$  with high accuracy (see lemma a.1), we can precisely compute  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  and  $w(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  by calling candidateset  $(x,\mathcal{h}_m,\beta_m,\alpha)$  and confwidth  $(x,\mathcal{h}_m,\beta_m,\alpha)$  with sufficiently small  $\alpha$ , which can be efficiently implemented with oracle $_{\beta/\alpha}$  (as confbound can be efficiently implemented with oracle $_{\beta/\alpha}$ ).

case 2: general function class. if  $\mathcal{f}$  is not a product function class, then it is impossible to maintain  $\mathcal{f}_m$  as a product function class. in this case, our implementation of candidateset uses both confbound and confbounddiff to approximately compute  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$ . we use the following observation.

lemma a.4. for any  $\mathcal{f}' \subset \mathcal{f}$ , for any  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ , define  $\widetilde{a} = \arg \max_{a \in \mathcal{a}} \sup_{f \in \mathcal{f}'} f(x, a)$ , and define

$$
\widehat {\mathcal {a}} (x; \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}) := \left\{a \in \mathcal {a}: \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} ^ {\prime}} (f (x, \widetilde {a}) - f (x, a)) \leq 0 \right\}.
$$

it holds that

1.  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})\subset \widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime}).$  
2. if  $|\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})| = 1$ , then,  $\mathcal{a}(\mathcal{f}',x) = \widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}') = \{\widetilde{a}\}$ .  
3.  $\mathbb{i}\{\left|\widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})\right| > 1\} = \mathbb{i}\{\left|\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})\right| > 1\}$  
4.  $w(x,\mathcal{f}^{\prime}) = \mathbb{i}\big\{| \widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})| > 1\big\} \max_{a\in \mathcal{a}}\big|\sup_{f\in \mathcal{f}^{\prime}}f(x,a) - \inf_{f\in \mathcal{f}^{\prime}}f(x,a)\big|$ .

parts 1 and 2 of lemma a.4 imply that, for a general function class  $\mathcal{f}$ , we are able to compute  $\widehat{\mathcal{a}}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  by calling confbounddiff $(x, \mathcal{h}_m, \beta_m, \alpha)$  with sufficiently small  $\alpha$ , which serves as an approximation to the true candidate action set  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$ , with the following two properties:

- the computed set  $\widehat{\mathcal{a}}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  always contain the true set  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$ .

- the computed set  $\widehat{\mathcal{a}}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  coincides with the true set  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  when  $|\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)| = 1$ .

parts 3 and 4 state two consequences of the above properties:

- though we may not be able to directly compute the candidate action set  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$ , we can always precisely compute the disagreement indicator  $\mathbb{i}\{|a(x;\mathcal{f}')| > 1\}$  by computing  $\mathbb{i}\{|\hat{a} (x;\mathcal{f}')| > 1\}$ .  
- as a result, we can always precisely compute the confidence width  $w(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  (which depends on  $\mathbb{i} \{|a(x; \mathcal{f}')| > 1\}$ ), irrespective of whether  $a(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  is precisely computed or note.

note that it is sufficient for adacb to use  $\widehat{\mathcal{a}}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  (rather than the true candidate action set  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$ ) and  $w(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  to obtain the near-optimal instance-dependent regret stated in theorem 2.1 and theorem 2.3. in particular, in the proofs of theorem 2.1 and theorem 2.3, if we replace  $\mathcal{a}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$  by its approximation  $\widehat{\mathcal{a}}(x; \mathcal{f}_m)$ , then all the arguments hold as long as the following three conditions hold:

1.  $\pi^{\star}(x)\in \widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  with high probability;  
2.  $\mathbb{i}\{\left|\widehat{a} (x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})\right| > 1\} = \mathbb{i}\{\left|\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}^{\prime})\right| > 1\}$  
3.  $\hat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}_m) = \mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  when  $|\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)| = 1$

the first condition holds because  $\mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)\subset \widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  and adacb guarantees that  $\pi^{\star}(x)\in \mathcal{a}(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$  with high probability. the second and third conditions are exactly  $\widehat{\mathcal{a}} (x;\mathcal{f}_m)$ 's properties. as a result, candidateset and confwidth work.

# a.1 deferred proofs

proof of lemma 4.1. recall that any element of  $\operatorname{star}(\mathcal{f},\widehat{f})$  can be written as  $t\cdot f + (1 - t)\widehat{f}$ , where  $f\in \mathcal{f}$  and  $t\in [0,1]$ . hence, for any  $\varepsilon >0$ , if we set  $n = \lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil$  and take  $\mathcal{i} = \{0,\varepsilon ,\dots ,(n - 1)\varepsilon \}$  as an  $\varepsilon$ -net for  $[0,1]$  and define  $\widetilde{\mathcal{f}} = \{tf' + (1 - t)\widehat{f}\mid f\in \mathcal{f},t\in \mathcal{i}\}$ , we have that  $\widetilde{\mathcal{f}}$ $\varepsilon$ -approximates  $\operatorname{star}(\mathcal{f},\widehat{f})$  pointwise. we can implement erm over  $\widetilde{\mathcal{f}}$  using  $1 / \varepsilon$  oracle calls. in particular, for each  $t\in \mathcal{i}$ , we simply solve

$$
\begin{array}{l} \underset {f \in \mathcal {f}} {\arg \min } \sum_ {(w, x, a, y) \in \mathcal {h}} w (t f (x, a) + (1 - t) \widehat {f} (x, a) - y) ^ {2} \tag {41} \\ = \operatorname *{arg  min}_{f\in \mathcal{f}}\sum_{(w,x,a,y)\in \mathcal{h}}wt^{1 / 2}\big(f(x,a) - (y - (1 - t)\widehat{f} (x,a)) / t\big)^{2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which can be seen as an instance  $\mathsf{oracle}_{b / \varepsilon}$ . letting  $f_{t}$  denote the solution above, we return  $f = tf_{t} + (1 - t)\widehat{f}$  for the  $(t,f_t)$  pair that minimizes (41). since all the arguments to the square loss in (41) are bounded by  $b$  and  $w\leq w$ , it is clear that this leads to an  $\mathcal{o}(bw\varepsilon)$ -approximate minimizer.

![](images/ee6716a28ee55aa790c7ee18acbd1ca8af4557dc540c6e626aa9df7940424f9b.jpg)

proof of lemma a.1. since

$$
f (x, a _ {1}) - f (x, a _ {2}) = \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, 1) + \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, 2) - \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} - \frac {1}{\alpha} - \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, 1) ^ {2} + f (x, 2) ^ {2}\right),
$$

we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} (f (x, a _ {1}) - f (x, a _ {2})) \leq \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \left\{\frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {1}) + \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {2}) - \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} \right\} - \frac {1}{\alpha}, \\ \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} (f (x, a _ {1}) - f (x, a _ {2})) \geq \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \left\{\frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {1}) + \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {2}) - \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} \right\} - \frac {1}{\alpha} - \alpha . \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, to compute  $\inf_{f\in \mathcal{f}_m}(f(x,a_1) - f(x,a_2))$  with to an error up to  $2\alpha$ , we only need to compute

$$
\inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \left\{\frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {1}) + \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} + \frac {\alpha}{2} \left(f (x, a _ {2}) - \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2} \right\}
$$

with an error up to  $\alpha$ . this can be efficiently accomplished through a binary search procedure similar to the procedures in foster et al. (2018) and krishnamurthy et al. (2017). this procedure requires access to  $\mathrm{oracle}_{\beta/\alpha}$ .

# part i

# proofs for contextual bandit results

# b technical tools

# b.1 concentration

lemma b.1 (freedman's inequality (e.g., agarwal et al. (2014))). let  $(z_{t})_{t\leq t}$  be a real-valued martingale difference sequence adapted to a filtration  $\mathfrak{g}_t$ , and let  $\mathbb{e}_t[\cdot ]\coloneqq \mathbb{e}[\cdot \mid \mathfrak{g}_t]$ . if  $|\bar{z}_t|\le r$  almost surely, then for any  $\eta \in (0,1 / r)$  it holds that with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ ,

$$
\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} \leq \eta \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbb {e} _ {t - 1} \left[ z _ {t} ^ {2} \right] + \frac {\log (\delta^ {- 1})}{\eta}.
$$

lemma b.2. let  $(x_{t})_{t\leq t}$  be a real-valued sequence of random variables adapted to a filtration  $\mathfrak{g}_t$ . if  $|x_{t}|\leq r$  almost surely, then with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ ,

$$
\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} x _ {t} \leq \frac {3}{2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbb {e} _ {t - 1} [ x _ {t} ] + 4 r \log (2 \delta^ {- 1}),
$$

and

$$
\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbb {e} _ {t - 1} \left[ x _ {t} \right] \leq 2 \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} x _ {t} + 8 r \log (2 \delta^ {- 1}).
$$

proof. immediate consequence of lemma b.1.

lemma b.3 (bernstein's inequality for  $[0,1]$ -valued random variables). let  $x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n}$  be i.i.d.  $[0,1]$ -valued random variables with mean  $\mu$  and variance  $\sigma^2$ . for any  $\delta >0$ , with probability at least  $1 - 2\delta$ ,

$$
\left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} x _ {i} - \mu \right| \leq \frac {1}{3 n} \log (2 \delta^ {- 1}) + \sqrt {\frac {2 \sigma^ {2} \log (2 \delta^ {- 1})}{n}} \leq \frac {1}{3 n} \log (2 \delta^ {- 1}) + \sqrt {\frac {2 \mu \log (2 \delta^ {- 1})}{n}}.
$$

proof. the first inequality follows from  $|x| \leq 1$  and bernstein's inequality for bounded random variables. the second inequality follows from  $x \in [0,1]$  and  $\sigma^2 = \mathbb{e}[x^2] - (\mathbb{e}[x])^2 \leq \mathbb{e}[x] - (\mathbb{e}[x])^2 \leq \mu$ .

# b.2 information theory
