\begin{array}{l} w _ {m} = \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}} [ w (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m}) ] \\ = \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left[ \mathbb {i} \{\left| \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m}) \right| > 1 \} \sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m})} \sup  _ {f, f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\prime} (x, a) | \right] \\ \leq \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left[ \mathbb {i} \{| a (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m}) | > 1 \} \sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m})} \left(\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | + \sup  _ {f ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f ^ {\prime} (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) |\right) \right] \\ = 2 \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left[ \mathbb {i} \left\{\left| \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m}) \right| > 1 \right\} \sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | \right] \\ \leq 2 \int_ {\delta / 2} ^ {1} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(\sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right) d \omega \\ \leq 2 \int_ {\delta / 2} ^ {1} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(\sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m - 1})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right) d \omega , \tag {68} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality utilizes  $\mathcal{f}_{m - 1}\subset \mathcal{f}_m$

let  $\omega > 0$  be fixed. for all  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ , the definition of  $p_{m-1}$  implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m - 1})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \mathbb {i} \left\{\left| f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) \right| > \omega \right\} \\ \leq \left(\sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m - 1}))} \frac {1}{p _ {m - 1} (a \mid x)}\right) \mathbb {e} _ {a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left[ \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \mathbb {i} \left\{\left| f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) \right| > \omega \right\} \right] \\ \leq (a + \gamma_ {m - 1}) \mathbb {e} _ {a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left[ \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \mathbb {i} \left\{| f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega \right\} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(\sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m - 1})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right) \\ = \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}} \left[ \sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m - 1})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \mathbb {i} \left\{\left| f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) \right| > \omega \right\} \right] \\ \leq (a + \gamma_ {m - 1}) \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left[ \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} \mathbb {i} \left\{| f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega \right\} \right] \\ = \left(a + \gamma_ {m - 1}\right) \mathbb {p} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left(\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right). \tag {69} \\ \end{array}
$$

by part 1 of lemma c.2, we have

$$
\forall f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}, \quad \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} (f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a)) ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon_ {m} ^ {2}.
$$

hence, for all  $\omega \in (0,1]$ , by the definition of  $\pmb{\theta}^{\mathrm{val}}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left(\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left(\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega , \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} (f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a)) ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon_ {m} ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \omega , \varepsilon_ {m}) \frac {\varepsilon_ {m} ^ {2}}{\omega^ {2}}. \tag {70} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining (68), (69), (70), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {m} \leq 2 \int_ {\delta / 2} ^ {1} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(\sup  _ {a \in \mathcal {a} (x; \mathcal {f} _ {m - 1})} \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right) d \omega \\ \leq 2 \left(a + \gamma_ {m - 1}\right) \int_ {\delta / 2} ^ {1} \mathbb {p} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}, a \sim p _ {m - 1} (\cdot | x)} \left(\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {f} _ {m}} | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \omega\right) d \omega \\ \leq 2 \left(a + \gamma_ {m - 1}\right) \int_ {\delta / 2} ^ {1} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \omega , \varepsilon_ {m}) \frac {\varepsilon_ {m} ^ {2}}{\omega^ {2}} d \omega \\ \leq 2 \left(a + \gamma_ {m - 1}\right) \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta / 2, \varepsilon_ {m}) \varepsilon_ {m} ^ {2} \int_ {\delta / 2} ^ {1} \frac {1}{\omega^ {2}} d \omega \\ = 4 \left(a + \gamma_ {m - 1}\right) \frac {\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \varepsilon_ {m}) \varepsilon_ {m} ^ {2}}{\delta}. \\ \end{array}
$$

we are now ready to state our instance-dependent regret bound, which is a best-of-both-worlds guarantee.

lemma c.22. for any  $\delta \in (t^{-2},1]$ ,  $c > 0$ , by setting  $\beta_{m} = (m - m + 1)c_{\delta}$  for all  $m \in [m]$ , algorithm 1 with option ii ensures that for any instance with uniform gap  $\delta > 0$ ,

$$
\overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} \leq \tilde {\mathcal {o}} (c + c ^ {- 1}) \cdot \max  \left\{\sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}, \frac {\theta^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} \right\}
$$

with probability at least  $1 - \delta$

proof. without loss of generality, we assume  $t$  is large enough such that  $\sqrt{at\log(|\mathcal{f}| / \delta)} \geq 65\log (4m / \delta)$  for all  $\delta \in (t^{-2},1]$  (otherwise,  $t$  must be upper bounded by an absolute constant, and we know that  $\overline{\mathrm{reg}}_t$  is upper bounded by the same constant).

by lemma c.1 and lemma c.4,  $\mathcal{e}$  and  $\mathcal{e}_{\mathrm{w}}$  simultaneously hold with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ . in the rest of the proof, we assume that both  $\mathcal{e}$  and  $\mathcal{e}_{\mathrm{w}}$  hold. by lemma c.2, the specification of  $\{\beta_m\}_{m=1}^t$  in algorithm 1 ensures that  $f^\star \in \mathcal{f}_m \subset \dots \subset \mathcal{f}_1$  whenever  $\mathcal{e}$  holds.

we consider two cases.

case 1. first, consider the case where

$$
\frac {\pmb {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} <   \frac {\sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}}{1 6 m c _ {\delta}}.
$$

since  $\beta_{m} = (m - m + 1)c_{\delta}$  for all  $m\in [m]$ , we have

$$
\frac {3 m c _ {\delta}}{n _ {m - 1}} \geq \frac {2 \beta_ {m} + c _ {\delta}}{n _ {m - 1}} \geq \frac {3 c _ {\delta}}{n _ {m - 1}}
$$

for all  $m \in [m]$ . in what follows, we prove that  $\gamma_1 = \dots = \gamma_m = 0$  via induction.

- base case: since  $\widehat{w}_1 = 1 < \sqrt{at\log(|\mathcal{f}| / \delta)} /n_0$  , we have  $\lambda_{1} = 0$  , thus  $\gamma_{1} = 0$  
- assume that  $\gamma_{m - 1} = 0$ . then by lemma c.21,

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {m} \leq 4 (a + \gamma_ {m - 1}) \cdot \frac {\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {\left(2 \beta_ {m} + c _ {\delta}\right) / n _ {m - 1}}\right)}{\delta} \cdot \frac {2 \beta_ {m} + c _ {\delta}}{n _ {m - 1}} \\ \leq 4 a \cdot \frac {\theta^ {\text {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right)}{\delta} \cdot \frac {3 m c _ {\delta}}{n _ {m - 1}} \\ <   \frac {3 \sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}}{4 n _ {m - 1}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

if  $w_{m}\geq 64\log (4m / \delta) / n_{m - 1}$ , then by (46),

$$
\widehat {w} _ {m} \leq \frac {4}{3} w _ {m} <   \frac {\sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}}{n _ {m - 1}},
$$

thus  $\lambda_{m} = \mathbb{i}\{\widehat{w}_{m}\geq \sqrt{at\log(|\mathcal{f}| / \delta)} /n_{m - 1}\} = 0$  , and  $\gamma_{m} = 0$  . if  $w_{m} <   64\log (4m / \delta) / n_{m - 1}$  , then by (46),

$$
\widehat {w} _ {m} <   6 5 \frac {\log (4 m / \delta)}{n _ {m - 1}} \leq \frac {\sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}}{n _ {m - 1}},
$$

thus  $\lambda_{m} = \mathbb{i}\{\widehat{w}_{m}\geq \sqrt{at\log(|\mathcal{f}| / \delta)} /n_{m - 1}\} = 0$  , and  $\gamma_{m} = 0$

- therefore,  $\gamma_{1} = \dots = \gamma_{m} = 0$ .

by lemma c.21, since  $\gamma_1 = \dots = \gamma_m = 0$ , we now have that for all  $m \in [m]$

$$
w _ {m} \leq 4 a \cdot \frac {\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right)}{\delta} \cdot \frac {3 m c _ {\delta}}{n _ {m - 1}}.
$$

by the definition of  $w(x;\mathcal{f}_m)$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} = \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbb {e} \left[ r _ {t} \left(\pi^ {\star} \left(x _ {t}\right)\right) - r _ {t} \left(a _ {t}\right) \mid \mathfrak {g} _ {t - 1} \right] \\ \leq \sum_ {m = 1} ^ {m} \sum_ {t = \tau_ {m - 1} + 1} ^ {\tau_ {m}} \mathbb {e} \left[ r _ {t} \left(\pi^ {\star} \left(x _ {t}\right)\right) - r _ {t} \left(a _ {t}\right) \mid \mathfrak {g} _ {\tau_ {m - 1}} \right] \\ \leq 2 \sum_ {m = 1} ^ {m} n _ {m} w _ {m} \\ \leq 4 8 m c _ {\delta} \sum_ {m = 1} ^ {m} \frac {\theta^ {\text {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {1 / t} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} \\ = 4 8 m ^ {2} c _ {\delta} \cdot \frac {\theta^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {1 / t} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta}. \\ \end{array}
$$

case 2. we now consider the case where

$$
\frac {\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} \geq \frac {\sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}}{1 6 m c _ {\delta}}.
$$

in this case, by lemma c.20, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (c + c ^ {- 1}) \cdot \sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)} \\ \leq \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (c + c ^ {- 1}) \cdot \left(1 6 m c _ {\delta} \cdot \frac {\theta^ {\text {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta}\right) \\ = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (c + c ^ {- 1}) \cdot \frac {\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta}. \\ \end{array}
$$

combining the above two cases with lemma c.20, we know that

$$
\overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} \leq \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (c + c ^ {- 1}) \cdot \max  \left\{\sqrt {a t \log (| \mathcal {f} | / \delta)}, \frac {\theta^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} \right\}.
$$

![](images/11c3ec36717c80bd05924f8652995b7eddfc3321a4d41d06ba0f674adc11dcc9.jpg)

theorem 2.3 can be directly obtained from lemma c.22: by taking  $\delta = 1 / t$  and  $c = 1$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \mathrm {r e g} _ {t} \right] = \mathbb {e} \left[ \overline {{\mathrm {r e g}}} _ {t} \right] = \widetilde {\mathcal {o}} (1) \cdot \frac {\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} \left(\mathcal {f} , \delta / 2 , \sqrt {3 c _ {\delta} / n _ {m - 1}}\right) a \log | \mathcal {f} |}{\delta} + \mathcal {o} (1),
$$

where the additional  $\mathcal{o}(1)$  term comes from the fact  $\overline{\mathrm{reg}}_t$  may violate the high-probability bound in lemma c.22 with probability at most  $1 / t$ .

# d proofs for lower bounds

for the proofs in this section, we let  $\mathfrak{g}_t = \sigma((x_1, a_1, r_1(a_1)), \ldots, (x_t, a_t, r_t(a_t)))$  be the natural filtration, and define

$$
\overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} := \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbb {e} \left[ r _ {t} \left(\pi^ {\star} \left(x _ {t}\right)\right) - r _ {t} \left(a _ {t}\right) \mid \mathfrak {g} _ {t - 1} \right]
$$

to be the sum of conditional expectations of the instantaneous regret. we define  $p_t(x, a)$  to be the algorithm's action distribution at time  $t$  when  $x_t = x$ , i.e.

$$
p _ {t} (x, a) = \mathbb {p} \left(a _ {t} = a \mid \mathfrak {g} _ {t - 1}, x _ {t} = x\right). \tag {71}
$$

we also define  $\bar{p} = \frac{1}{t}\sum_{t=1}^{t}p_t$  to be the average action distribution (or, the result of applying online-to-batch-conversion to the algorithm).

for functions  $p:\mathcal{x}\times \mathcal{a}\to \mathbb{r}$ , we define  $\| p\|_{l_1(\mathcal{d})} = \mathbb{e}_{x\sim \mathcal{d}}\big[\sum_{a\in \mathcal{a}}|p(x,a)|\big]$ . we overload this notation for policies  $\pi :\mathcal{x}\rightarrow \mathcal{a}$  in the natural way by writing  $\pi (x,a) = \mathbb{i}\{\pi (x) = a\}$ .

# d.1 basic technical results

the following lemmas are used in multiple proofs in this section.

lemma d.1. consider a fixed context distribution  $\mathcal{d}$  and bayes reward function  $f^{\star}$ . suppose that  $f^{\star}$  has gap  $\delta$  almost surely. let  $p_t(x, a)$  denote the contextual bandit algorithm's action probability for action  $a$  given context  $x$  at time  $t$  (i.e.,  $p_t(x, a) = \mathbb{p}(a_t = a \mid \mathfrak{g}_{t-1}, x_t = x)$ ), and let  $\bar{p}(x, a) = \frac{1}{t} \sum_{t=1}^{t} p_t(x, a)$ . then

$$
\overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} \geq \frac {\delta}{2} t \cdot \| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \mathbb {e} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \frac {\delta}{2} t \cdot \mathbb {e} \| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})}.
$$

proof of lemma d.1. this result follows by using the uniform gap property, then repeatedly invoking jensen's inequality.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} \geq \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \delta \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}} \mathbb {e} _ {a \sim p _ {t} (x)} \mathbb {i} \left\{a _ {t} \neq \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} (x) \right\} \\ \geq \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \frac {\delta}{2} \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}} \mathbb {e} _ {a \sim p _ {t} (x)} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} \left| e _ {a _ {t}} - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} (x, a) \right| \\ \geq \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \frac {\delta}{2} \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} | p _ {t} (x, a) - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} (x, a) | \\ \geq \frac {\delta}{2} t \mathbb {e} _ {x \sim \mathcal {d}} \sum_ {a \in \mathcal {a}} | \bar {p} (x, a) - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} (x, a) | \\ = \frac {\delta}{2} t \| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})}. \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/22bb0cb555482ed5a805e5e7791d272627411ce9a218dd368a2a19b549b6efdd.jpg)

lemma d.2 (fano method with reverse kl-divergence (raginsky and rakhlin, 2011)). let

$$
\mathcal {h} = \left(x _ {1}, a _ {1}, r _ {1} \left(a _ {1}\right)\right), \dots , \left(x _ {t}, a _ {t}, r _ {t} \left(a _ {t}\right)\right),
$$

and let  $\{\mathbb{p}^{(i)}\}_{i\in [m]}$  be a collection of measures over  $\mathcal{h}$ , where  $m\geq 2$ . let  $\mathbb{q}$  be any fixed reference measure over  $\mathcal{h}$ , and let  $\mathbb{p}$  be the law of  $(m^{\star},\mathcal{h})$  under the following process:

- sample  $m^{\star} \sim [m]$  uniformly.  
sample  $\mathcal{h}\sim \mathbb{p}^{(m^{\star})}$

then for any function  $\widehat{m} (\mathcal{h})$ , if  $\mathbb{p}(\widehat{m} = m^{\star})\geq 1 - \delta$ , then

$$
\frac {1}{2} \log (1 / \delta) - \log 2 \leq \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}).
$$

proof. this is established in raginsky and rakhlin (2011), but we re-prove the lemma in detail for completeness.

we begin with an intermediate result for general  $f$ -divergences. let  $\phi : [0, \infty) \to \mathbb{r}$  be any convex function for which  $\phi(1) = 0$ . for measures  $\mathbb{p}$  and  $\mathbb{q}$  over any measurable space  $\omega$  which are dominated by a  $\sigma$ -finite measure  $\mu$ , define

$$
d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} \parallel \mathbb {q}) = \int \frac {d \mathbb {q}}{d \mu} \phi \left(\frac {d \mathbb {p} / d \mu}{d \mathbb {q} / d \mu}\right) d \mu ,
$$

and let

$$
d _ {\phi} (p \parallel q) = q \phi (p / q) + (1 - q) \phi ((1 - p) / (1 - q)).
$$

let  $\mathbb{p}$  be as in the theorem statement, and let  $\mathbb{p}_0 = \mathrm{unif}([m])\otimes \mathbb{q}$ . then, with  $z = \mathbb{i}\{\widehat{m} = m^{\star}\}$ ,

$$
d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} \parallel \mathbb {p} _ {0}) \stackrel {(i)} {\geq} d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} | _ {z} \parallel \mathbb {p} _ {0} | _ {z}) \stackrel {(i i)} {=} d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} (z = 1) \parallel \mathbb {p} _ {0} (z = 1)),
$$

where  $\mathbb{p}|_{z}$  is the law of  $z$  under  $\mathbb{p}$ . here  $(i)$  is the standard data processing inequality for  $f$ -divergences (e.g., raginsky and rakhlin (2011)) and  $(ii)$  follows because  $z$  is binary.

we choose  $\phi (u) = -\log (u)$ . then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} (z = 1) \parallel \mathbb {p} _ {0} (z = 1)) = d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} (z = 1) \parallel 1 / m) \\ = \frac {1}{m} \phi (\mathbb {p} (z = 1) / (1 / m)) + (1 - 1 / m) \phi (\mathbb {p} (z = 0) / (1 - 1 / m)) \\ = (1 - 1 / m) \log (1 / \mathbb {p} (z = 0)) - \frac {1}{m} \log (\mathbb {p} (z = 1) \cdot (1 / m)) - (1 - 1 / m) \log (1 / (1 - 1 / m)) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(i)} {\geq} \frac {1}{2} \log (1 / \mathbb {p} (z = 0)) - \frac {1}{m} \log ((1 / m)) - (1 - 1 / m) \log (1 / (1 - 1 / m)) \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {\geq} \frac {1}{2} \log (1 / \mathbb {p} (z = 0)) - \log 2 \\ \geq \frac {1}{2} \log (1 / \delta) - \log 2, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $(i)$  uses that  $m\geq 2$  and that  $\mathbb{p}(z = 1)\leq 1$ , and  $(ii)$  uses that the entropy of a binary random variable is at most log 2.

finally, we observe that since  $\mathbb{p}(m,\mathcal{h}) = \frac{1}{m}\mathbb{p}^{(m)}(\mathcal{h})$  and  $\mathbb{p}_0(m,\mathcal{h}) = \frac{1}{m}\mathbb{q}(\mathcal{h})$

$$
d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} \parallel \mathbb {p} _ {0}) = \mathbb {e} _ {m \sim \mathrm {u n i f} (m)} [ d _ {\phi} (\mathbb {p} ^ {(m)} \parallel \mathbb {q}) ] = \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \parallel \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}).
$$

![](images/41b35ba93d8e6e065ab3bbdbe6f8b8929969f68e2b86159b15a57a8d5c065e8e.jpg)

lemma d.3. let  $\{f^{(i)},\ldots ,f^{(m)}\}$  be a collection of regression functions, all with uniform gap at least  $\delta$  and let  $\mathcal{d}\in \delta (\mathcal{x})$ . let  $\mathbb{p}^{(i)}$  be the law of  $\mathfrak{g}_t$  for an arbitrary contextual bandit instance in which  $\mathcal{d}$  is the context distribution and  $f^{(i)}$  is the bayes reward function, and let  $\mathbb{e}_i[\cdot ]$  denote the expectation under  $\mathbb{p}^{(i)}$ . let  $\mathbb{q}$  be a fixed reference measure. suppose the regression functions form a  $2\varepsilon$ -packing with respect to  $\mathcal{d}$  in the sense that

$$
\left\| \pi_ {f ^ {(i)}} - \pi_ {f ^ {(j)}} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \geq 2 \varepsilon \forall i \neq j. \tag {72}
$$

then any algorithm that ensures that  $\mathbb{e}_i[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\varepsilon \delta t}{32}$  for all  $i$  must have

$$
\log 2 \leq \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}). \tag {73}
$$

proof. using lemma d.1, for all choices  $f^{\star} = f^{(i)}$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {i} \left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \leq \frac {2}{\delta t} \mathbb {e} _ {i} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ].
$$

in particular, by markov's inequality, this implies that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {i} \left(\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} > \varepsilon\right) \leq \frac {2}{\delta \varepsilon t} \mathbb {e} _ {i} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ]
$$

hence, if  $\mathbb{e}_i[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta\varepsilon t}{32}$  for all  $i$ , we have  $\mathbb{p}_i\left(\| \bar{p} - \pi_{f^{\star}} \|_{l_1(\mathcal{d})} > \varepsilon\right) \leq \frac{1}{2}$ . since  $\left\| \pi_{f^{(i)}} - \pi_{f^{(i')}} \right\|_{l_1(\mathcal{d})} > 2\varepsilon$  for all  $i \neq i'$ , this implies that for any choice  $f^{\star} = f^{(i)}$ , with probability at least  $1 - 1/16$  over the data generating process,

$$
\hat {i} := \underset {j} {\arg \min } \left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {(j)}} \right\|
$$

has  $\hat{i} = i$ . indeed, conditioned on the event above, we have  $\left\| \bar{p} - \pi_{f^{(i)}} \right\| \leq \varepsilon$ , and the packing property implies that

$$
\left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {(i ^ {\prime})}} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \geq \left\| \pi_ {f ^ {(i)}} - \pi_ {f ^ {(i ^ {\prime})}} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} - \left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {(i)}} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} > 2 \varepsilon - \left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {f ^ {(i)}} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \geq \varepsilon
$$

for all  $i' \neq i$ . hence, applying lemma d.2 with  $\delta = 1/16$ , we have that for any reference measure  $\mathbb{q}$

$$
\log 2 = \frac {1}{2} \log (1 / \delta) - \log 2 \leq \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}).
$$

# d.2 proof of theorem 2.2 and theorem 2.4

the roadmap for this proof is as follows. first, we construct a family of hard instances as a function of the parameters in theorem 2.2 and show that it leads to the lower bound in terms of the policy disagreement coefficient. then, at the end of the theorem, we show how the same construction immediately implies theorem 2.4 using a different choice of problem parameters.

**construction.** let us take  $\mathcal{a} = \{a^{(1)}, a^{(2)}, \ldots, a^{(a)}\}$  to be an arbitrary set of discrete actions. let  $k := \lfloor \theta \rfloor \leq 1 / \varepsilon$  and (recall that  $\theta \geq 1$ , so that  $k \geq 1$  as well) let  $d$  be a parameter of the construction to be chosen momentarily. we define  $\mathcal{x} \subseteq \mathbb{n}$  based on  $d$  disjoint partitions  $\mathcal{x}^{(1)}, \ldots, \mathcal{x}^{(d)}$ . we set  $\mathcal{x}^{(1)} = \{x^{(1,0)}, x^{(1,1)}, \ldots, x^{(1,k)}\}$ ,  $\mathcal{x}^{(2)} = \{x^{(2,0)}, x^{(2,1)}, \ldots, x^{(2,k)}\}$ , and so forth, where  $\{x^{(i,j)}\}$  is an arbitrary collection of distinct contexts of size  $d \cdot (k + 1)$ . we set  $\mathcal{x} = \bigcup \mathcal{x}^{(i)}$ .

for each partition  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$ , we take  $\pi^{(i)} \subseteq (\mathcal{x}^{(i)} \to \mathcal{a})$  to be a collection of policies  $\{\pi^{(i,l,b)}\}$  where, for each  $l \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$  and  $b \in \mathcal{a}_0 := \mathcal{a} \setminus \{a^{(1)}\}$ , we have  $\pi^{(i,l,b)}(x^{(i,0)}) = a^{(1)}$  and

$$
\pi^ {(i, l, b)} (x ^ {(i, j)}) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} a ^ {(1)}, & j \neq l, \\ b, & j = l, \end{array} \right.
$$

we also include a policy  $\pi^{(i,0)}$  that always selects  $a^{(1)}$ . we define  $\pi$  obtained by stitching together  $\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(d)}$  over their respective subsets of the domain. the resulting policy class consists of all policies which deviate from  $a^{(1)}$  on a subset of contexts of size at most  $d$ , and for which this subset intersects with each  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$  at most once.

we now choose a regression function class  $\mathcal{f}$  that induces  $\pi$ . for each subset  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$  we define a class of regression functions  $\mathcal{f}^{(i)}: \mathcal{x}^{(i)} \to [0,1]$  as follows. first, we let  $f^{(i,0)}(x^{(i,j)},\cdot) = \mu_0 := (1/2 + \delta, 1/2, \ldots, 1/2)$  for all  $j$ . next, for each  $b \in \mathcal{a}_0$  let

$$
\mu_ {b} = \left(^ {1} / 2 + \delta , \ldots , \underbrace {1 / 2 + 2 \delta} _ {\text {c o o r d i n a t e} b}, \ldots , ^ {1} / 2\right),
$$

with the  $1/2 + 2\delta$  entry on the  $b$ th coordinate. next, for each  $l \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$  and  $b \in \mathcal{a}_0$  we let

$$
f ^ {(i, l, b)} (x ^ {(i, j)}, \cdot) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} (1 / 2 + \delta , 1 / 2, \ldots , 1 / 2), & \quad j \neq l, \\ \mu_ {b}, & \quad j = l. \end{array} \right.
$$

as with  $\pi$ , we obtain  $\mathcal{f}$  by stitching together  $\mathcal{f}^{(1)}, \ldots, \mathcal{f}^{(d)}$  over their respective subsets of the domain. it is easily verified that  $\pi$  is precisely the set of argmax policies for  $\mathcal{f}$ .

we define the context distribution  $\mathcal{d}$  as follows:

- let  $\mathcal{d}^{(i)}$  be the distribution over  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$  which takes each of  $x^{(i,1)},\ldots ,x^{(i,k)}$  with probability  $\varepsilon$  and takes  $x^{(i,0)}$  with probability  $1 - k\varepsilon \geq 0$ .  
let  $\mathcal{d} = \frac{1}{d}\sum_{i = 1}^{d}\mathcal{d}^{(i)}$

verifying the problem parameters. we now choose the parameter  $d$  and verify that  $|\mathcal{f}|, \theta^{\mathrm{pol}}$ , and  $\theta^{\mathrm{val}}$  are bounded appropriately. we first observe that since each value function  $f \in \mathcal{f}$  deviates from the vector  $\mu_0$  on at most  $d$  contexts, and since it can switch to one of the  $a - 1$  vectors  $\{\mu_b\}_{b \in \mathcal{a}_0}$  each such context,

$$
| \mathcal {f} | \leq \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {d} \binom {d (k + 1)} {i} \cdot (a - 1) ^ {i} \leq a ^ {d} \cdot \left(\frac {e d (k + 1)}{d}\right) ^ {d} = (e ^ {2} a k) ^ {d}.
$$

we choose  $d$  to be the largest possible value such that  $(e^2 ak)^d \leq f$ ; this is possible by the assumption that  $\theta \leq e^{-2}f / a$ . since  $(e^2 ak)^{d + 1} \geq f$ , we have

$$
d \geq \frac {\log f}{2 \log \left(e ^ {2} a / \varepsilon\right)} \geq \omega (1) \cdot \frac {\log f}{\log \left(a / \varepsilon\right)},
$$

where the last expression uses that  $\varepsilon \leq 1$  and  $a \geq 2$ . hence, going forward, we focus our attention to lower bounding the regret in terms of  $d$ , which is equivalent to  $\log f$  up to logarithmic factors.

to verify that the conditions of theorem 2.2 hold, we show that the policy disagreement coefficient is bounded by  $\theta$  for our construction. observe that for any fixed policy  $\pi^{\star} \in \pi$ ,  $\mathbb{p}_{\mathcal{d}}(\exists \pi \in \pi : \pi(x) \neq \pi^{\star}(x)) \leq k\varepsilon$ , since for any block  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$ , all policies agree on  $x^{(i,0)}$ . hence, if we define  $\pi_{\varepsilon}(\pi^{\star}) = \{\pi \in \pi : \mathbb{p}_{\mathcal{d}}(\pi(x) \neq \pi^{\star}(x)) \leq \varepsilon\}$  we have that for all  $\varepsilon' \geq \varepsilon$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(\exists \pi \in \pi_ {\varepsilon^ {\prime}} \left(\pi^ {\star}\right): \pi (x) \neq \pi^ {\star} (x)\right) \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(\exists \pi \in \pi : \pi (x) \neq \pi^ {\star} (x)\right) \leq k \varepsilon \leq k \varepsilon^ {\prime}.
$$

it follows that  $\pmb{\theta}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi, \varepsilon) \leq k \leq \theta$  for any choice of  $\pi^{\star}$ . since  $\theta \geq 1$ , we also have  $k \geq \theta / 2$ .

the lower bound. for each  $i, l, b$ , let  $\mathbb{p}_r^{(i,l,b)}$  denote the reward distribution given by  $r(a) \sim \mathrm{ber}(f^{(i,l,b)}(x,a))$  conditioned on  $x$  for each  $x \in \mathcal{x}^{(i)}$ . note that  $b$  is ignored if  $l = 0$ , so in this case we may abbreviate to  $\mathbb{p}_r^{(i,0)}$ .

for any sequence  $\alpha = \alpha_{1},\ldots ,\alpha_{d}$ , where  $\alpha_{i} = (v_{i},b_{i})$  for  $v_{i}\in \{0,1,\dots ,k\}$  and  $b_{i}\in \{2,\dots ,a\}$ , we let  $\mathbb{p}_{\alpha}$  denote the law of  $\mathcal{h}:= (x_1,a_1,r_1(a_1)),\ldots ,(x_t,a_t,r_t(a_t))$  when the loss distribution for  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$  is given by  $\mathbb{p}_r^{(i,v_i,b_i)}$ . we sample the problem instance  $\alpha$  from a distribution  $\nu$  defined as follows:

- for each  $i$ , set  $v_{i} = 0$  with probability  $1/2$ . otherwise, select  $v_{i}$  uniformly from  $\{1, \ldots, k\}$ . select  $b_{i}$  uniformly from  $\{2, \ldots, a\}$ .

note that when  $v_{i} = 0$  we disregard the value of  $b_{i}$ . let  $\pi_{\alpha}$  denote the optimal policy under  $\alpha$ , and let  $\pi_{\alpha}^{(i)}$  denote its restriction to  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$ .

let  $p_t(x, a)$  be the algorithm's action distribution at time  $t$ , as in (71). let  $\mathbb{e}_{\alpha}[\cdot]$  denote the expectation under  $\mathbb{p}_{\alpha}$ . since the bayes reward function has uniform gap  $\delta$  for each choice of  $\alpha$ , lemma d.1 implies that

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] \geq \frac {\delta}{2} t \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} \| \bar {p} - \pi_ {\alpha} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})},
$$

where  $\bar{p} = \frac{1}{t}\sum_{t=1}^{t} p_t$ . moreover, we have

$$
\left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {\alpha} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} = \frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \left\| \bar {p} - \pi_ {\alpha} ^ {(i)} \right\| _ {l _ {1} \left(\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)}\right)},
$$

where  $\pi_{\alpha}^{(i)}$  is the restriction of  $\pi_{\alpha}$  to  $\mathcal{x}^{(i)}$ .

now, for each  $(v,b)$  for  $v\in \{1,\dots ,k\}$  and  $b\in \mathcal{a}$ , let  $\mathbb{p}^{(i,v,b)} = \mathbb{e}_{\alpha \sim \nu}[\mathbb{p}_\alpha \mathbb{i}\{v_i = v,b_i = b\} ]$ , and let  $\mathbb{p}^{(i,0)}\mathbb{e}_{\alpha \sim \nu}[\mathbb{p}_\alpha \mathbb{i}\{v_i = 0\} ]$  then for each  $i$ , the inequalities above imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {\nu \sim \alpha} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \frac {\delta}{2} \frac {t}{d} \cdot \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha \sim \nu} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} \| \bar {p} - \pi_ {\alpha} ^ {(i)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})} \\ = \frac {\delta}{2} \frac {t}{d} \cdot \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \left(\frac {1}{2} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} \| \bar {p} - \pi^ {(i, 0)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})} + \frac {1}{2 k a _ {0}} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {b \in \mathcal {a} _ {0}} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, l, b)}} \| \bar {p} - \pi^ {(i, l, b)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $a_0 \coloneqq a - 1$ . in particular, we conclude that

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\nu \sim \alpha} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \frac {\delta}{4} \frac {t}{d} \cdot \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{k a _ {0}} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {b \in \mathcal {a} _ {0}} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, l, b)}} \| \bar {p} - \pi^ {(i, l, b)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})}. \tag {74}
$$

let  $\mathcal{i} \subseteq [d]$  denote the set of indices  $i$  for which

$$
\frac {1}{k a _ {0}} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {b \in \mathcal {a} _ {0}} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, l, b)}} \| \bar {p} - \pi^ {(i, l, b)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})} \leq \frac {\varepsilon}{3 2}. \tag {75}
$$

we consider two cases. first, if  $|\mathcal{i}| \leq d/2$ , then (74) implies that

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\nu \sim \alpha} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq 2 ^ {- 8} \cdot \varepsilon \delta t,
$$

so we are done. for the other case, we have  $|\mathcal{i}| \geq d / 2$ , and we argue that the algorithm must solve a hypothesis test for each index in this set. let  $i \in \mathcal{i}$  be fixed. first, observe that for any  $(l,b) \neq (l',b')$ , we have

$$
\left\| \pi^ {(i, l, b)} - \pi^ {(i, l ^ {\prime}, b ^ {\prime})} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})} \geq \varepsilon
$$

consider the markov chain in which we draw  $(l,b)\sim \mathrm{unif}([k]\times [a_0])$ , then take  $\mathcal{h}\sim \mathbb{p}^{(i,l,b)}$ . letting  $\mathbb{p}_i$  be the law under this process, (75) implies that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {i} \left(\left\| \bar {p} - \pi^ {(i, l, b)} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d} ^ {(i)})} > \varepsilon / 2\right) \leq \frac {1}{1 6}
$$

by markov's inequality. hence, if we define  $(\hat{l},\hat{b}) = \arg \min_{l^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\big\| \bar{p} -\pi^{(i,l^{\prime},b^{\prime})}\big\|_{l_1(\mathcal{d}^{(i)})}$ , we have that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {i} \left(\left(\hat {i}, \hat {b}\right) = (i, b)\right) \geq 1 - 1 / 1 6.
$$

applying lemma d.2, this implies that for any reference measure  $\mathbb{q}$  over  $\mathcal{h}$ ,

$$
\log 2 \leq \frac {1}{k a _ {0}} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {b \in a _ {0}} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i, l, b)}).
$$

we choose  $\mathbb{q} = \mathbb{p}^{(i,0)}$ . now note that under the distributions  $\mathbb{p}^{(i,l,b)}$  and  $\mathbb{p}^{(i,0)}$ , the feedback the algorithm receives in a given round is identical unless both a)  $x_{t} = x^{(i,l)}$  and b) the algorithm selects action  $b$ . hence, using the usual likelihood ratio argument, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i, l, b)}\right) = d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\operatorname {b e r} (1 / 2) \| \operatorname {b e r} (1 / 2 + 2 \delta)\right) \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} [ | \{t \mid x _ {i} = l, a _ {t} = b \} | ] \\ \leq 4 \delta^ {2} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} [ | \{t \mid x _ {i} = l, a _ {t} = b \} | ], \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used lemma b.5 and that  $\delta \leq 1 / 4$ . thus, taking the average, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \log 2 \leq 4 \delta^ {2} \frac {1}{k a _ {0}} \sum_ {l = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {b \in \mathcal {a} _ {0}} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} [ | \{t \mid x _ {t} = x ^ {(i, l)}, a _ {t} = b \} | ] \\ = 4 \delta^ {2} \frac {1}{k a _ {0}} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} [ | \{t \mid x _ {t} \in \mathcal {x} ^ {(i)} \backslash x ^ {(i, 0)}, a _ {t} \neq a ^ {(1)} \} | ], \\ \end{array}
$$

or in other words,

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} \left[ \left| \left\{t \mid x _ {t} \in \mathcal {x} ^ {(i)} \backslash x ^ {(i, 0)}, a _ {t} \neq a ^ {(1)} \right\} \right| \right] \geq \frac {\log 2}{4} \frac {k a _ {0}}{\delta^ {2}}. \tag {76}
$$

clearly, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha \sim \nu} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \delta \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha \sim \nu} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {i} \left\{x _ {t} \in \mathcal {x} ^ {(i)} \backslash x ^ {(i, 0)}, a _ {t} \neq a ^ {(1)}, v _ {i} = 0 \right\} \right] \\ \geq \frac {\delta}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} ^ {(i, 0)}} [ | \{t \mid x _ {t} \in \mathcal {x} ^ {(i)} \backslash x ^ {(i, 0)}, a _ {t} \neq a ^ {(1)} \} | ]. \\ \end{array}
$$

since we have assumed that  $|\mathcal{i}|\geq \frac{d}{2}$ , this expression combined with (76) implies that

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\alpha \sim \nu} \mathbb {e} _ {\alpha} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \frac {\log 2}{8} \cdot \frac {a _ {0} k d}{\delta} = \widetilde {\omega} (1) \cdot \frac {\theta a \log f}{\delta}.
$$

this proves theorem 2.2.

deducing theorem 2.4. to prove theorem 2.4 with parameters  $a$ ,  $f$ ,  $\delta$ ,  $\varepsilon$ , and  $\theta$ , we apply the construction above with parameter  $\varepsilon_0 \coloneqq \frac{\varepsilon^2}{\delta^2}$ , which is admissible for any choice of  $\theta \leq 1 / \varepsilon_0 \wedge e^{-2} a / f$ . since we have already shown that this construction ensures that any algorithm has

$$
\mathbb {e} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \widetilde {\omega} (1) \cdot \min  \left\{\varepsilon_ {0} \delta t, \frac {\theta a \log f}{\delta} \right\}
$$

for some instance, all that remains is to verify that  $\theta^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon)\leq \theta$

for any fixed  $f^{\star}\in \mathcal{f}$  , we have

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} (\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta / 2) \leq k \varepsilon_ {0},
$$

since all of the value functions in  $\mathcal{f}$  agree on  $x^{(i,0)}$  for all  $i$ . furthermore, since  $|f(x,a) - f^{\star}(x,a)| \leq \delta$  for all  $f \in \mathcal{f}$ , we also have

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} (\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta^ {\prime}) \leq 0
$$

for all  $\delta' \geq \delta$ . it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sup  _ {\delta^ {\prime} \geq \delta / 2, \varepsilon^ {\prime} \geq \varepsilon} \frac {\delta^ {\prime 2}}{\varepsilon^ {\prime 2}} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta^ {\prime}, \| f - f ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \leq \varepsilon^ {\prime}\right) \\ \leq \sup  _ {\delta^ {\prime} \geq \delta / 2, \varepsilon^ {\prime} \geq \varepsilon} \frac {\delta^ {\prime 2}}{\varepsilon^ {\prime 2}} k \varepsilon_ {0} \mathbb {i} \{\delta^ {\prime} <   \delta \} \\ \leq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{\varepsilon^ {2}} k \varepsilon_ {0} \leq k, \\ \end{array}
$$

so that  $\pmb{\theta}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon)\leq k\leq \theta$

![](images/75f593227c003c6444284ce90a814f7365f3eadcc54fd673afe8fef53e363b83.jpg)

# d.3 proof of theorem 2.6

rather than proving theorem 2.6 directly, in this section we first state a more general theorem which implies it, then prove this theorem. to state the stronger theorem, we recall the definition of the graph dimension which is a multiclass analogue of the vc dimension.

definition d.1 (graph dimension (natarajan, 1989)). the graph dimension  $d_{\mathcal{g}}(\pi, \pi^{\star})$  is the largest number  $d$  such that there exists  $s \subseteq \mathcal{x}$  with  $|s| = d$  such that for all  $t \subseteq s$ , there exists  $\pi \in \pi$  such that

$$
\pi (x) = \pi^ {\star} (x) \forall x \in t, \quad a n d \quad \pi (x) \neq \pi^ {\star} (x) \forall x \in s \backslash t. \tag {77}
$$

theorem d.1 (full version of theorem 2.6). let a policy class  $\pi$ ,  $\pi^{\star} \in \pi$ , and  $\delta \in (0,1/8)$  be given. then there exist  $\mathcal{f}$ ,  $\mathcal{d}$ , and  $f^{\star} \in \mathcal{f}$  with  $\pi_{f^{\star}} = \pi^{\star}$  such that the following properties hold:

-  $\{\pi_f \mid f \in \mathcal{f}\} \subseteq \pi$ .  
- any algorithm with  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta t}{32\underline{s}_{\pi^*}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)}$  for all instances realizable by  $\mathcal{f}$  must have

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] \geq \frac {\mathfrak {s} _ {\pi^ {\star}} ^ {\mathrm {p o l}} (\pi)}{8 \delta} \tag {78}
$$

for some instance in which  $f^{\star}$  is the bayes reward function.

- for any algorithm with  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta t}{16}$  for all instances realizable by  $\mathcal{f}$ , there exists a realizable instance for which

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] \geq \frac {d _ {\mathcal {g}} \left(\pi , \pi^ {\star}\right)}{6 4 \delta}. \tag {79}
$$

proof of theorem d.1. let  $(x^{(1)},a^{(1)}),\ldots ,(x^{(n)},a^{(n)})$  witness the strong star number, and let us abbreviate  $z^{(i)} = (x^{(i)},a^{(i)})$ . let  $x^{(i_1)},\ldots ,x^{(i_m)}$  denote the unique contexts above, and note that  $m\leq \underline{\mathfrak{s}}_{\pi^{\star}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)$ . let  $y^{(1)},\ldots ,y^{(d)}\in \mathcal{x}$  witness the graph number  $d_{\mathcal{g}}(\pi ,\pi^{\star})$ . we choose  $\mathcal{d}$  to select  $x$  from  $\mathrm{unif}(x^{(i_1)},\dots,x^{(i_m)})$  with probability  $1 / 2$  and  $\mathrm{unif}(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(d)})$  with probability  $1 / 2$ .

define

$$
f ^ {(0)} (x, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {\star} (x), \\ 1 / 2, & \quad a \neq \pi^ {\star} (x). \end{array} \right.
$$

we choose  $f^{\star} = f^{(0)}$  as the reference regression function in the theorem statement.

part 1: star number. let  $\pi^{(1)},\ldots ,\pi^{(n)}$  be the policies accompanying  $z^{(1)},\ldots ,z^{(n)}$  that witness the strong star number. for each  $i$ , define a regression function  $f^{(i)}$  to have  $f^{(i)}(x^{(j)},a) = f^{(0)}(x^{(j)},a)$  for all  $x\neq x^{(i)}$ , and let

$$
f ^ {(i)} (x ^ {(i)}, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {\star} (x), \\ 1 / 2 + 2 \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {(i)} (x), \\ 1 / 2, & \quad \text {o t h e r w i s e}. \end{array} \right.
$$

for  $x \notin \{x^{(1)}, \ldots, x^{(n)}\}$ , we simply define

$$
f ^ {(i)} (x, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {(i)} (x), \\ 1 / 2, & \quad \text {o t h e r w i s e}. \end{array} \right.
$$

lemma d.4. for all  $i$ , we have  $\pi^{(i)} = \pi_{f^{(i)}}$ .

proof. let  $i$  be fixed. for  $x \notin \{x^{(1)}, \ldots, x^{(n)}\}$  the result is immediate. for  $x^{(j)} \neq x^{(i)}$ , definition 2.2 requires that  $\pi^{(i)}(x^{(j)}) = \pi^{\star}(x^{(j)})$ , and we have  $\pi_{f^{(i)}}(x^{(j)}) = \pi_{f^{\star}}(x^{(j)}) = \pi^{\star}(x^{(j)})$ . finally, we have  $\pi_{f^{(i)}}(x^{(i)}) = \pi^{(i)}(x^{(i)})$  by construction.

observe that all of the regression functions have uniform gap  $\delta$ . moreover, for all  $i \neq j$  for  $i, j \geq 1$ , we have

$$
\left\| \pi^ {(i)} - \pi^ {(j)} \right\| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} = \frac {2}{m}.
$$

for each  $0 \leq i \leq m$ , we define an instance with law  $\mathbb{p}^{(i)}$  by taking  $\mathcal{d}$  as the context distribution and  $r(a) \sim \mathrm{ber}(f^{(i)}(x, a)) \mid x$ . let  $\mathbb{e}_i[\cdot]$  denote the expectation under this instance. then by lemma d.3, any algorithm that has  $\mathbb{e}_i[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta t}{32m}$  for all  $1 \leq i \leq n$  must have

$$
\log 2 \leq \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} ^ {(0)} \parallel \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}\right).
$$

we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} ^ {(0)} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}\right) \leq \mathbb {e} _ {0} \left[ \left| \left\{t: \left(x _ {t}, a _ {t}\right) = \left(x ^ {(i)}, a ^ {(i)}\right) \right\} \right| \right] \cdot d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\operatorname {b e r} \left(1 / 2 + \delta\right) \| \operatorname {b e r} \left(1 / 2 + 2 \delta\right)\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {e} _ {0} [ | \{t: (x _ {t}, a _ {t}) = (x ^ {(i)}, a ^ {(i)}) \} | ] \cdot 4 \delta^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

as long as  $\delta \leq 1/8$  (by lemma b.5). since the tuples  $(x^{(i)}, a^{(i)})$  are distinct, this implies that

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {0} | \{t: a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (x _ {t}) \} | \geq n \frac {\log 2}{4 \delta^ {2}}.
$$

finally, we use that since  $f^{(0)}$  has uniform gap  $\delta$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {0} [ \mathrm {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \delta \mathbb {e} _ {0} | \{t: a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (x _ {t}) \} | \geq n \frac {\log 2}{4 \delta} \geq \frac {n}{8 \delta}.
$$

part 2: graph number. for each  $v \in \{\pm 1\}^d$ , let  $\pi_v \in \pi$  be such that  $\pi_v(y^{(i)}) = \pi^\star(y^{(i)})$  if  $v_i = 1$  and  $\pi_v(y^{(i)}) \neq \pi^\star(y^{(i)})$  if  $v_i = -1$ ; these policies are guaranteed to exist by the definition of the graph number. let  $f^{(0)}$  be defined as before, and for each  $v$  define

$$
f _ {v} (y ^ {(i)}, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}), \\ 1 / 2 + 2 \delta , & \quad a = \pi_ {v} (y ^ {(i)}), \quad v _ {i} = - 1, \\ 1 / 2, & \quad \text {o t h e r w i s e}. \end{array} \right.
$$

for each  $i$ . for  $x \notin \{y^{(1)}, \ldots, y^{(d)}\}$ , define

$$
f _ {v} (x, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & a = \pi_ {v} (x), \\ 1 / 2, & \mathrm {o t h e r w i s e .} \end{array} \right.
$$

for each  $v$ , let  $\mathbb{p}_v$  denote the law of instance with  $\mathcal{d}$  as the context distribution and  $r(a)\sim \mathrm{ber}(f_v(x,a))\mid x$ , and let  $\mathbb{e}_v[\cdot ]$  denote the expectation under this distribution.

our starting point is the following lemma.

lemma d.5. with our choice of  $\mathcal{d}$ , for any bayes reward function  $f^{\star}$  with gap  $\delta$ , we have

$$
\overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} \geq \frac {\delta t}{8 d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {i} \left\{\bar {p} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) <   1 / 2 \right\}.
$$

proof. we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\operatorname {r e g}}} _ {t} \geq \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left(x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}\right) \sum_ {a} p _ {t} \left(y ^ {(i)}, a\right) \left(f ^ {\star} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) - f ^ {\star} \left(y ^ {(i)}, a\right)\right) \\ \geq \frac {1}{2 d} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \sum_ {a} p _ {t} \left(y ^ {(i)}, a\right) \left(f ^ {\star} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) - f ^ {\star} \left(y ^ {(i)}, a\right)\right) \\ \geq \frac {\delta}{4 d} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \| p _ {t} (y ^ {(i)}, \cdot) - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} (y ^ {(i)}, \cdot) \| _ {1} \\ \geq \frac {\delta t}{4 d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \| \bar {p} (y ^ {(i)}, \cdot) - \pi_ {f ^ {\star}} (y ^ {(i)}, \cdot) \| _ {1} \\ \geq \frac {\delta t}{8 d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {i} \left\{\bar {p} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi_ {f ^ {*}} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) <   1 / 2 \right\}. \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/ea818a9e14e793bf0ef623a7b87345cec2f0e533ec647eaf6e09f93246174e61.jpg)

using lemma d.5, for any  $v$  we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {v} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] \geq \frac {\delta t}{8 d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {p} _ {v} \left(\bar {p} _ {t} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi_ {v} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) <   1 / 2\right) \\ \geq \frac {\delta t}{8 d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {p} _ {v} (\bar {p} _ {t} (y ^ {(i)}, \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)})) <   1 / 2 \wedge v _ {i} = + 1) + \mathbb {p} _ {v} (\bar {p} _ {t} (y ^ {(i)}, \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)})) > 1 / 2 \wedge v _ {i} = - 1). \\ \end{array}
$$

in particular, suppose we sample  $v \sim \{\pm 1\}^d$  uniformly at random. then, if we let  $\mathbb{p}_{+i} = \frac{1}{2^d}\sum_{v \in \mathcal{v}: v_i = 1} \mathbb{p}_v$  and  $\mathbb{p}_{-i} = \frac{1}{2^d}\sum_{v \in \mathcal{v}: v_i = -1} \mathbb{p}_v$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] \geq \frac {\delta t}{8 d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \mathbb {p} _ {+ i} \left(\bar {p} _ {t} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi^ {\star} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) <   1 / 2\right) + \mathbb {p} _ {- i} \left(\bar {p} _ {t} \left(y ^ {(i)}, \pi^ {\star} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right)\right) > 1 / 2\right).
$$

by the definition of the total variation distance, we can lower bound this by

$$
\frac {\delta t}{8} \left(1 - \frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \left\| \mathbb {p} _ {+ i} - \mathbb {p} _ {- i} \right\| _ {1}\right).
$$

in particular, suppose that  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta t}{16}$ . then, rearranging, we have

$$
\frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \left\| \mathbb {p} _ {+ i} - \mathbb {p} _ {- i} \right\| _ {1} \geq \frac {1}{2}.
$$

for each  $v$ , let  $\mathbb{p}_{v, +i}$  and  $\mathbb{p}_{v, -i}$  denote the law when the  $i$ th coordinate of  $v$  is forced to  $+1$  and  $-1$ , respectively. then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \| \mathbb {p} _ {+ i} - \mathbb {p} _ {- i} \| \leq \left(\frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \| \mathbb {p} _ {+ i} - \mathbb {p} _ {- i} \| _ {1} ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \leq \left(\frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}} \| \mathbb {p} _ {v, + i} - \mathbb {p} _ {v, - i} \| _ {1} ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq 2 ^ {1 / 2} \left(\frac {1}{d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {v, + i} \| \mathbb {p} _ {v, - i}\right)\right) ^ {1 / 2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is pinsker. rearranging, we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {v, + i} \| \mathbb {p} _ {v, - i}\right) \geq \frac {d}{8}.
$$

for any fixed  $v$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {v, + i} \| \mathbb {p} _ {v, - i}\right) \leq \mathbb {e} _ {v, + i} [ | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}) \} | ] \cdot d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\operatorname {b e r} \left(1 / 2 + \delta\right) \| \operatorname {b e r} \left(1 / 2 + 2 \delta\right)\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {e} _ {v, + i} [ | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}) \} | ] \cdot 4 \delta^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\delta \leq 1 / 8$  (by lemma b.5). as a result, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {v, + i} \| \mathbb {p} _ {v, - i}\right) \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}} \mathbb {e} _ {v, + i} [ | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}) \} | ] \cdot 4 \delta^ {2} \\ \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}, v _ {i} = + 1} \mathbb {e} _ {v, + i} [ | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}) \} | ] \cdot 8 \delta^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

rearranging, this gives

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v \in \mathcal {v}, v _ {i} = + 1} \mathbb {e} _ {v, + i} [ | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}) \} | ] \geq \frac {d}{6 4 \delta^ {2}}.
$$

finally, observe that for any  $v$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {v} \left[ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} \right] \geq \delta \mathbb {e} _ {v} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbb {i} \left\{a _ {t} \neq \pi_ {v} \left(x _ {t}\right) \right\} \right] \geq \delta \mathbb {e} _ {v} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} | \left\{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi_ {v} \left(y ^ {(i)}\right) \right\} | \right] \\ \geq \delta \mathbb {e} _ {v} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}), v _ {i} = + 1 \} | \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

hence, under  $v$  drawn from the uniform distribution, we have

$$
\mathbb {e} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] = \mathbb {e} _ {v \sim \text {u n i f}} \mathbb {e} _ {v} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \delta \cdot \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{2 ^ {d}} \sum_ {v: v _ {i} = + 1} \mathbb {e} _ {v} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} | \{t: x _ {t} = y ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} \neq \pi^ {\star} (y ^ {(i)}) \} | \right] \geq \frac {d}{6 4 \delta}.
$$

![](images/f1370c76af83c871793b641d5cbc686eeed36d2b775c441eb4358dacf83a29a9.jpg)

# d.4 proof of theorem 2.8

let  $\delta \in (0,1)$  and  $f^{\star} \in \mathcal{f}$  be given. let  $t$  be fixed and recall that  $\varepsilon_t$  is chosen as the largest value such that

$$
\varepsilon_ {t} ^ {2} t \leq \underline {{\mathfrak {s}}} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta / 2, \varepsilon_ {t}). \tag {80}
$$

if  $\underline{\mathfrak{s}}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon_t) = 0$  the theorem is trivial, so let us consider the case where  $m\equiv \underline{\mathfrak{s}}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon_t)\geq 1$ . let  $x^{(1)},\ldots ,x^{(m)}$  and  $f^{(1)},\ldots ,f^{(m)}$  witness the star number, and let  $f^{(0)} = f^{\star}$ . define a family of contextual bandit instances with law  $\mathbb{p}^{(i)}$  for  $0\leq i\leq m$  via:

- take the context distribution  $\mathcal{d}$  to be uniform over  $\{x_{1},\ldots ,x_{m}\}$ .  
- choose  $r(a) \sim \mathcal{n}(f^{(i)}(x,a),1)\mid x$

we let  $\mathbb{e}_i[\cdot ]$  denote the expectation under  $\mathbb{p}^{(i)}$ . we draw the true instance (which we denote by  $0\leq v\leq m$ ) from a distribution  $\nu$  defined as follows: choose  $v = 0$  with probability  $1 / 2$  and  $v$  uniform from  $\{1,\dots ,m\}$  otherwise.

let  $\pi^{(i)}$  denote the optimal policy for instance  $i$ . since  $f^{\star}$  has gap  $\delta$  for every context, the conditions characterizing the star number ensure the following.

lemma d.6. for all  $1 \leq i \leq m$ ,

1.  $f^{(i)}$  has gap  $\frac{\delta}{2}$  over  $\{x_1,\ldots ,x_m\}$  
2.  $\pi^{(i)}(x^{(j)}) = \pi^{(0)}(x^{(j)})$  for all  $i\neq j$  
3.  $\pi^{(i)}(x^{(i)})\neq \pi^{(0)}(x^{(i)})$

proof. the third item is immediate. for the first item, we have two cases. first, for  $x^{(i)}$ , that  $f^{(i)}$  has gap  $\delta / 2$  is immediate from item 1 of definition 2.4. for  $j \neq i$ , item 3 ensures that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f ^ {(i)} \left(x ^ {(j)}, \pi^ {\star} \left(x ^ {(j)}\right) \geq f ^ {\star} \left(x ^ {(j)}, \pi^ {\star} \left(x ^ {(j)}\right) - \varepsilon \right. \right. \\ \geq \max  _ {a \neq \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)})} f ^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)}, a) + \delta - \varepsilon \geq \max  _ {a \neq \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)})} f (x ^ {(j)}, a) + \delta - 2 \varepsilon . \\ \end{array}
$$

in particular, since  $\varepsilon \leq \delta /4$ , we have

$$
f ^ {(i)} (x ^ {(j)}, \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)}) \geq \max  _ {a \neq \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)})} f (x ^ {(j)}, a) + \frac {\delta}{2}.
$$

this also implies the second item.

![](images/20cee02d948a8daec988af2a111421e69cab19610f557694a828bc2d7c26b5f5.jpg)

now to begin, observe that we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {v \sim \nu} \mathbb {e} _ {v} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \geq \max  \left\{\frac {1}{2} \mathbb {e} _ {0} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ], \frac {1}{2 m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {e} _ {i} [ \operatorname {r e g} _ {t} ] \right\}. \tag {81}
$$

let  $c$  be a constant, and assume that  $\mathbb{e}_{v\sim \nu}\mathbb{e}_v[\mathrm{reg}_t]\leq c\frac{\delta t}{m}$ . then by lemma d.1 and (81), we have

$$
\frac {\delta}{8} t \cdot \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {e} _ {i} \| \bar {p} _ {t} - \pi^ {(i)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \leq c \frac {\delta t}{m},
$$

or

$$
\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {e} _ {i} \| \bar {p} _ {t} - \pi^ {(i)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} \leq 8 c \frac {1}{m}.
$$

in particular, choosing  $c = \frac{1}{64}$ , this implies that

$$
\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {p} _ {i} \left(\| \bar {p} _ {t} - \pi^ {(i)} \| _ {l _ {1} (\mathcal {d})} > 2 / m\right) \leq \frac {1}{1 6}.
$$

since the policies form a  $\frac{4}{m}$ -packing in the sense that  $\| \pi^{(i)} - \pi^{(j)}\|_{l_1(\mathcal{d})} \geq \frac{4}{m}$  for  $i \neq j$ , this implies that we can identify the true policies with probability at least  $1 - 1/16$  over the random draw of  $v$  (conditioned on  $v \neq 0$ ). hence, applying lemma d.2, we have that for any reference measure  $\mathbb{q}$ ,

$$
\log 2 = \frac {1}{2} \log (1 6) - \log 2 \leq \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \| \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}).
$$

we choose  $\mathbb{q} = \mathbb{p}^{(0)}$ . defining  $n_{i} = |\{t : x_{t} = x^{(i)}, ; a_{t} \neq \pi^{(0)}(x^{(i)})\}|$  and  $n = |\{t : a_{t} \neq \pi^{\star}(x_{t})\}|$ , we use item 2 and item 3 of definition 2.4 to compute

$$
\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {q} \parallel \mathbb {p} ^ {(i)}) \leq \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2} \mathbb {e} _ {0} [ n _ {i} ] + \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{2} \frac {t}{m} = \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2 m} \mathbb {e} _ {0} [ n ] + \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{2} \frac {t}{m}.
$$

in particular, the choice for  $\varepsilon_t$  in (24) ensures that  $\varepsilon_t^2 t / m\leq 1$ . hence, rearranging, we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {0} [ n ] \geq \frac {2 m}{\delta^ {2}} (\log 2 - 1 / 2) \geq \frac {m}{4 \delta^ {2}}.
$$

since  $\mathbb{e}_0[\mathrm{reg}_t] \geq \delta \mathbb{e}_0[n_0]$ , this implies that  $\mathbb{e}_0[\mathrm{reg}_t] \geq \frac{m}{4\delta}$ .

# d.5 proof of theorem 2.9

let  $\delta \in (0,1)$  and  $f^{\star} \in \mathcal{f}$  be given. we consider instances defined by a value function  $f \in \mathcal{f}$  and sequence  $x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}$ , in which the contexts in the sequence are presented one-by-one non-adaptively and rewards are drawn as  $r_t(a) \sim \mathcal{n}(f(x_t,a),1)$ . for each such  $(f,x_{1:t})$  pair, we let

$$
\bar {r} (f, x _ {1: t}) = \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} r _ {t} \left(\pi_ {f} \left(x _ {t}\right)\right) - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} r _ {t} \left(a _ {t}\right) \mid f, x _ {1: t} \right]
$$

and

$$
r (f, x _ {1: t}) = \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} f (x _ {t}, \pi_ {f} (x _ {t})) - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} f (x _ {t}, a _ {t})
$$

be the sum of conditional-expected instantaneous regrets under this process, which is a random variable.

for each  $t$ , we let  $d_{t} = \underline{\mathfrak{e}}_{f}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta /2,\varepsilon_{t})$ , where we recall that  $\varepsilon_t$  is chosen such that

$$
\varepsilon_ {t} ^ {2} t \leq \underline {{\mathrm {e}}} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {f}, \delta / 2, \varepsilon_ {t}). \tag {82}
$$

in particular, it will be useful to note that  $d_{t}$  is non-increasing with  $t$ , so that  $d_{t} \leq t$  for  $t$  sufficiently large.

fix  $t$  sufficiently large such that  $d \leq t$ . let  $x^{(1)}, \ldots, x^{(d)}$  and  $f^{(1)}, \ldots, f^{(d)}$  realize the eluder dimension, and let  $f^{(0)} = f^{\star}$ . let  $\pi^{(0)}, \ldots, \pi^{(d)}$  be the induced policies. we have the following result.

lemma d.7. for all  $1 \leq i \leq d$ ,

1.  $f^{(i)}$  has gap  $\frac{\delta}{2}$  over  $\{x_{1},\ldots ,x_{i}\}$  
2.  $\pi^{(i)}(x^{(j)}) = \pi^{(0)}(x^{(j)})$  for all  $j < i$ .  
3.  $\pi^{(i)}(x^{(i)})\neq \pi^{(0)}(x^{(i)})$

![](images/7bd295836c26a169583ec16021fce64c634b172e567c628883586b92a9418e65.jpg)

let  $x_{t} = \{x_{1},\ldots ,x_{t}\}$  denote the sequence that plays  $x^{(1)}$  for the first  $\lfloor t / d\rfloor$  rounds,  $x^{(2)}$  for the second  $\lfloor t / d\rfloor$  rounds, and so forth, and choose an arbitrary fixed context to fill out the remaining rounds. let  $x_{t}^{(i)}$  denote the subsequence consisting of the first  $i$  blocks of contexts. let  $\mathcal{i}_i\subset [t]$  denote the rounds within the  $i$ th block. set  $m = \lfloor t / d\rfloor$ .

let the index  $i$  be fixed, and let  $n_t^{(i)} = |\{t \in \mathcal{i}_i \mid a_t \neq \pi^*(x^{(i)})\}|$  be the number of times the algorithm deviates from  $\pi^*$  in block  $i$  when the sequence is  $x_t$ . then we have

$$
r \left(f ^ {\star}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) \geq \delta \frac {m}{2} \mathbb {i} \left\{n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \geq m / 2 \right\},
$$

which follows from the fact that  $f^{\star}$  has gap  $\delta$ , and

$$
r \left(f ^ {(i)}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) \geq \delta \frac {m}{4} \mathbb {i} \left\{n _ {t} ^ {(i)} <   m / 2 \right\},
$$

which follows from the first part of lemma d.7 (i.e.,  $\pi^{\star}$  is  $\delta /2$ -suboptimal under  $f^{(i)}$  on context  $x^{(i)}$ ).

let  $\tau_{i}$  denote the last round in block  $i$ , and let  $\mathbb{p}_f^{(i)}$  denote the law of  $(a_1,\ldots ,a_{\tau_i})$ ,  $(r_1(a_1),\ldots ,r_{\tau_i}(a_{\tau_i}))$  when  $f$  is the mean reward function and  $x_{t}^{(i)}$  is the context sequence. with  $a = \{n_t^{(i)}\geq m / 2\}$ , the development above implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \bar {r} \left(f ^ {\star}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) + \bar {r} \left(f ^ {(i)}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) \geq \delta \frac {m}{4} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} (a) + \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)} \left(a ^ {\mathrm {c}}\right)\right) \\ \geq \delta \frac {m}{8} \exp \left(- d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \mid \mid \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}\right)\right), \tag {83} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is by lemma b.4. in particular, if the algorithm has  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{t}{64d}\delta \leq \frac{m}{32}\delta$  for all sequences of length  $t$  that are realizable by  $\mathcal{f}$ , then this implies that

$$
\log 2 \leq d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \| \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}\right).
$$

using item 1 and item 2 of definition 2.6, as well as the fact that rewards are gaussian, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \| \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}\right) \leq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2} \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)}} \left[ n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \right] + \sum_ {j <   i} \frac {t}{d} \cdot \frac {1}{2} \max  (f ^ {(i)} (x ^ {(j)}, a) - f ^ {\star} (x ^ {(j)}, a)) ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2} \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)}} \left[ n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \right] + \frac {t}{2 d} \varepsilon_ {t} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

our choice of  $\varepsilon_t$  ensures that  $\varepsilon_t^2 t / d\leq 1$ . it follows that

$$
d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \| \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}\right) \leq \frac {1}{2} \delta^ {2} \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)}} \left[ n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \right] + \frac {1}{2}. \tag {84}
$$

combining this inequality with (83) and rearranging, we get

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {*}} ^ {(i)}} \left[ n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \right] \geq \frac {2}{\delta^ {2}} \left(\log 2 - \frac {1}{2}\right).
$$

importantly, if we let  $\mathbb{p}_{f^{\star}}$  denote the full law of  $(a_{1},\ldots ,a_{t}),(r_{1}(a_{1}),\ldots ,r_{t}(a_{t}))$  under  $f^{\star}$  and  $x_{t}$ , this is equivalent to

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {*}}} \left[ n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \right] \geq \frac {2}{\delta^ {2}} \left(\log 2 - \frac {1}{2}\right) \geq \frac {1}{4 \delta^ {2}}.
$$

since  $n_t^{(i)}$  is a measurable function of the data up to and including the  $i$ th block. finally, under  $f^{\star}$ , we have

$$
\overline {{r}} (f ^ {\star}, x _ {t}) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \delta \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}}} \left[ n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \right] \geq \frac {d}{4 \delta^ {2}}.
$$

![](images/93bb4b6677b596dcc7cbaaf3fcdbcd1e564e4ecd47dc9711ad26c6eb799b09e5.jpg)

# d.6 proof of theorem 2.11

fix  $t\geq \mathfrak{e}_{\pi^{\star}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)$  and let  $(x^{(1)},a^{(1)}),\ldots ,(x^{(m)},a^{(m)})$  and  $\pi^{(1)},\dots,\pi^{(n)}$  witness the policy eluder dimension. define

$$
f ^ {(0)} (x, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {\star} (x), \\ 1 / 2, & \quad a \neq \pi^ {\star} (x). \end{array} \right.
$$

we choose  $f^{\star} = f^{(0)}$  as the reference regression function in the theorem statement. for each  $1 \leq i \leq n$ , define a regression function  $f^{(i)}$  to have  $f^{(i)}(x^{(j)},a) = f^{(0)}(x^{(j)},a)$  for all  $j < i$  with  $x^{(j)} \neq x^{(i)}$ , and set

$$
f ^ {(i)} (x ^ {(i)}, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {\star} (x ^ {(i)}), \\ 1 / 2 + 2 \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {(i)} (x ^ {(i)}), \\ 1 / 2, & \quad \text {o t h e r w i s e} \end{array} \right.
$$

finally, for all  $x \notin \{x^{(1)}, \ldots, x^{(i)}\}$ , set

$$
f ^ {(i)} (x, a) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 / 2 + \delta , & \quad a = \pi^ {(i)} (x), \\ 1 / 2, & \quad \text {o t h e r w i s e}. \end{array} \right.
$$

clearly we have  $\pi^{(i)} = \pi_{f^{(i)}}$ . we consider instances defined by a value function  $f \in \mathcal{f}$  and sequence  $x_1, \ldots, x_t$ , in which the contexts in the sequence are presented one-by-one non-adaptively and rewards are drawn as  $r_t(a) \sim \mathrm{ber}(f(x_t, a))$ . for each such  $(f, x_{1:t})$  pair, we let

$$
\bar {r} (f, x _ {1: t}) = \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} r _ {t} \left(\pi_ {f} \left(x _ {t}\right)\right) - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} r _ {t} \left(a _ {t}\right) \mid f, x _ {1: t} \right]
$$

and

$$
r (f, x _ {1: t}) = \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} f (x _ {t}, \pi_ {f} (x _ {t})) - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} f (x _ {t}, a _ {t}),
$$

with the actions in the latter quantity tacitly understood to depend on rewards drawn under  $r_t(a) \sim \mathrm{ber}(f(x_t, a))$ .

let  $x_{t} = \{x_{1},\ldots ,x_{t}\}$  denote the sequence that plays  $x^{(1)}$  for the first  $\lfloor t / m\rfloor$  rounds,  $x^{(2)}$  for the second  $\lfloor t / m\rfloor$  rounds, and so forth, and choose an arbitrary fixed context to fill out the remaining rounds. let  $x_{t}^{(i)}$  denote the subsequence consisting of the first  $i$  blocks of contexts. let  $\mathcal{i}_i\subset [t]$  denote the rounds within the  $i$ th block. set  $m = \lfloor t / m\rfloor$ .

let the index  $i$  be fixed, and let  $n_t^{(i)} = |\{t \in \mathcal{i}_i \mid a_t \neq \pi^*(x^{(i)})\}|$  be the number of times the algorithm deviates from  $\pi^{\star}$  in block  $i$  when the sequence is  $x_t$ . then we have

$$
r \left(f ^ {\star}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) \geq \delta \frac {m}{2} \mathbb {i} \left\{n _ {t} ^ {(i)} \geq m / 2 \right\},
$$

and

$$
r \left(f ^ {(i)}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) \geq \delta \frac {m}{2} \mathbb {i} \left\{n _ {t} ^ {(i)} <   m / 2 \right\},
$$

since both instances have uniform gap  $\delta$  over block  $i$ .<sup>23</sup>

let  $\tau_{i}$  denote the last round in block  $i$ , and let  $\mathbb{p}_f^{(i)}$  denote the law of  $(a_1, r_1(a_1)), \ldots, (a_{\tau_i}, r_{\tau_i}(a_{\tau_i}))$  when  $f$  is the mean reward function and  $x_{t}^{(i)}$  is the context sequence. with  $a = \left\{n_t^{(i)} \geq m / 2\right\}$ , the inequalities above imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \bar {r} \left(f ^ {\star}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) + \bar {r} \left(f ^ {(i)}, x _ {t} ^ {(i)}\right) \geq \delta \frac {m}{2} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} (a) + \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)} \left(a ^ {\mathrm {c}}\right)\right) \\ \geq \delta \frac {m}{4} \exp \left(- d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \| \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}\right)\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is by lemma b.4. in particular, if the algorithm has  $\mathbb{e}[\mathrm{reg}_t] \leq \frac{\delta t}{32m} \leq \frac{\delta m}{16}$  for all sequences of length  $t$  that are realizable by  $\mathcal{f}$ , then this implies that

$$
\log 2 \leq d _ {\mathrm {k l}} \left(\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \| \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}\right).
$$

now, since  $f^{\star}$  and  $f^{(i)}$  agree on  $x^{(j)}$  for all  $j < i$  with  $x^{(j)} \neq x^{(i)}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)} \parallel \mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {(i)}} ^ {(i)}) \leq d _ {\mathrm {k l}} (\mathrm {b e r} (1 / 2 + \delta) \parallel \mathrm {b e r} (1 / 2 + 2 \delta)) \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)}} [ | \{t \leq \tau_ {i}: x _ {t} = x ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} = a ^ {(i)} \} | ] \\ \leq 4 \delta^ {2} \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)}} [ | \{t \leq \tau_ {i}: x _ {t} = x ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} = a ^ {(i)} \} | ] \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\delta \leq 1 / 8$ . rearranging, we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}} ^ {(i)}} [ | \{t \leq \tau_ {i}: x _ {t} = x ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} = a ^ {(i)} \} | ] \geq \frac {1}{8 \delta^ {2}}.
$$

if we let  $\mathbb{p}_{f^{\star}}$  denote the full law of  $(a_{1},\ldots ,a_{t}),(r_{1}(a_{1}),\ldots ,r_{t}(a_{t}))$  under  $f^{\star}$  and  $x_{t}$ , this is equivalent to

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}}} [ | \{t \leq \tau_ {i}: x _ {t} = x ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} = a ^ {(i)} \} | ] \geq \frac {1}{8 \delta^ {2}}
$$

since  $|\{t \leq \tau_i : x_t = x^{(i)}, a_t = a^{(i)}\}|$  is a measurable function of the data up to and including the  $i$ th block. finally, since this argument holds for all  $1 \leq i \leq m$ , under  $f^{\star}$  we have

$$
\bar {r} (f ^ {\star}, x _ {t}) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \delta \mathbb {e} _ {\mathbb {p} _ {f ^ {\star}}} [ | \{t: x _ {t} = x ^ {(i)}, a _ {t} = a ^ {(i)} \} | ] \geq \frac {m}{8 \delta}.
$$
