# e additional proofs from section 2

# e.1 proofs for examples

proof of proposition 2.1. we first prove the bound for linear function classes let  $p:\mathcal{x}\to \delta (\mathcal{a})$  be fixed and define  $\sigma = \mathbb{e}_{\mathcal{d},p}\big[\phi (x,a)\phi (x,a)^{\top}\big]$ . let  $\mathcal{w}^{\star}:= \mathcal{w} - w^{\star}$ , where  $f^{\star}(x,a) = \langle w^{\star},\phi (x,a)\rangle$ . then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta , \| f - f ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \\ = \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists w \in \mathcal {w} ^ {\star}: | \langle w, \phi (x, a) \rangle | > \delta , \langle w, \sigma w \rangle \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\sup  _ {w: \langle w, \sigma w \rangle \leq \varepsilon^ {2}} | \langle w, \phi (x, a) \rangle | > \delta\right) \\ = \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\left\langle \phi (x, a), \sigma^ {\dagger} \phi (x, a) \right\rangle \geq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{\varepsilon^ {2}}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used that  $\phi (x,a)\in \mathrm{im}(\sigma)$  almost surely. by markov's inequality, we can upper bound

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\left\langle \phi (x, a), \sigma^ {\dagger} \phi (x, a) \right\rangle \geq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{\varepsilon^ {2}}\right) \leq \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{\delta^ {2}} \cdot \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left[ \left\langle \phi (x, a), \sigma^ {\dagger} \phi (x, a) \right\rangle \right].
$$

finally, we use that  $\mathbb{e}_{\mathcal{d},p}\big[\langle \phi (x,a),\sigma^{\dagger}\phi (x,a)\rangle \big] = \mathrm{tr}(\sigma \sigma^{\dagger})\leq d.$

to bound the value function disagreement coefficient for the general case, we use that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta , \| f - f ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \\ = \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \big (\exists w \in \mathcal {w}: | \sigma (\langle w, \phi (x, a) \rangle) - \sigma (\langle w ^ {\star}, \phi (x, a) \rangle) | > \delta , \| \sigma (\langle w, \phi (\cdot) \rangle) - \sigma (\langle w ^ {\star}, \phi (\cdot) \rangle) \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \big) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists w \in \mathcal {w}: c _ {u} | \langle w - w ^ {\star}, \phi (x, a) \rangle | > \delta , c _ {l} ^ {2} \| \langle w - w ^ {\star}, \phi (\cdot) \rangle \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \\ = \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists w \in \mathcal {w} ^ {\star}: | \langle w, \phi (x, a) \rangle | > \delta / c _ {u}, \langle w, \sigma w \rangle \leq \varepsilon^ {2} / c _ {l} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

from here, we proceed exactly as in the linear case to get the result.

proof of proposition 2.6. let  $\mathcal{w}^{\star} = \left\{w\in \mathbb{r}^{d}\mid \| w\|_{0}\leq 2s\right\}$ . then for any  $\delta ,\varepsilon >0$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists f \in \mathcal {f}: | f (x, a) - f ^ {\star} (x, a) | > \delta , \| f - f ^ {\star} \| _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \leq \varepsilon\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists w \in \mathcal {w} ^ {\star}: | \langle w, \phi (x, a) \rangle | > \delta , \alpha \langle w, \sigma^ {\star} w \rangle \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {d}, p} \left(\exists w \in \mathcal {w} ^ {\star}: | \langle w, \phi (x, a) \rangle | > \delta , \alpha \lambda_ {\mathrm {r e}} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {i} \left\{\exists w \in \mathcal {w} ^ {\star}: \| w \| _ {1} > \delta , \alpha \lambda_ {\mathrm {r e}} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \right\} \\ \leq \mathbb {i} \left\{\exists w \in \mathcal {w} ^ {\star}: \sqrt {2 s} \| w \| _ {2} > \delta , \alpha \lambda_ {\mathrm {r e}} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \right\} \\ \leq \mathbb {i} \left\{\frac {2 s \varepsilon^ {2}}{\alpha \lambda_ {\mathrm {r e}}} > \delta^ {2} \right\} \\ \leq \frac {2 s}{\alpha \lambda_ {\mathrm {r e}}} \cdot \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{\delta^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

since this holds for all choices of  $\delta$  and  $\varepsilon$ , the result is established.

# e.2 proofs for star number results

proof of proposition 2.2. let  $\delta \in (0,2/3)$  be fixed. let  $\mathcal{x} = [d]$  and  $\mathcal{a} = \{0,1\}$ . set  $f^{\star}(x,0) = \frac{1}{2}\delta$  and  $f^{\star}(x,1) = \delta$  for all  $x$ . for each  $i$ , define a function  $f_{i}$  as follows.

$f_{i}(x,1) = \delta$  for all  $x$  
$f_{i}(i,0) = \frac{3}{2}\delta .$  
$f_{i}(j,0) = 0$  for all  $j\neq i$

let  $\mathcal{f} = \{f^{\star}, f_1, \ldots, f_d\}$ . clearly we have  $\mathfrak{s}_{\pi^{\star}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(\pi) = d$ , since for each  $i$ ,  $\pi_{f_i}(i) \neq \pi^{\star}(i)$ , and  $\pi_{f_i}(j) = \pi^{\star}(j)$  for all  $j \neq i$ .

now, consider the value function star number. observe that for any  $i$ , and for any set of points  $\mathcal{i} \subseteq [d]$ , we have

$$
\sum_ {j \in \mathcal {i} \backslash \{i \}} (f _ {i} (j, 0) - f ^ {\star} (j, 0)) ^ {2} \geq \frac {\delta^ {2}}{4} (| \mathcal {i} | - 1)
$$

since any  $f_{i}$  has  $|f_{i}(x,a) - f^{\star}(x,a)| \geq \delta$  only if  $x = i$  and  $a = 0$ , we conclude the following:

1.  $\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{f\star}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta^{\prime}) = 0$  for all  $\delta^{\prime}\geq \delta$  
2.  $\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta^{\prime})\leq 5$  for all  $\delta^\prime <  \delta$  , since we must have  $\frac{\delta^2}{4} (|\mathcal{i}| - 1)\leq (\delta ')^2$  for any set  $\mathcal{l}$  that witnesses the star number.

it follows that  $\mathfrak{s}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta^{\prime})\leq 5$  for all  $\delta^\prime$

# e.2.1 proof of theorem 2.7

we prove a slightly more general version of theorem 2.7. consider a setting in which we have a function class  $\mathcal{g}:\mathcal{z}\to [0,1]$  and distribution  $\mathcal{p}\in \delta (\mathcal{z})$ . we introduce the following generalizations of the value function disagreement coefficient and value function star number. define

$$
\theta_ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta_ {0}, \varepsilon_ {0}) = \sup  _ {\delta > \delta_ {0}, \varepsilon > \varepsilon_ {0}} \frac {\delta^ {2}}{\varepsilon^ {2}} \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p}} \left(\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right), \tag {85}
$$

where  $\| g\|_{\mathcal{p}}^2 = \mathbb{e}_{\mathcal{p}}[g^2]$ . let  $\check{\mathfrak{s}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{g},\delta)$  be the length of the longest sequence of points  $z^{(1)},\ldots ,z^{(m)}$  such that for all  $i$ , there exists  $g^{(i)}\in \mathcal{g}$  such that

$$
g ^ {(i)} (z ^ {(i)}) > \delta , \quad \text {a n d} \quad \sum_ {j \neq i} (g ^ {(i)}) ^ {2} (z ^ {(j)}) \leq \delta^ {2}.
$$

the value function star number is defined as  $\mathfrak{s}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{g},\delta_0) = \sup_{\delta >\delta_0}\check{\mathfrak{s}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{g},\delta)$ .

our goal will be to prove the following result.

theorem e.1. for any uniform glivenko-cantelli class  $\mathcal{g} \subseteq (\mathcal{z} \to [0,1])$

$$
\sup  _ {\mathcal {p}} \sup  _ {\varepsilon > 0} \theta_ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta , \varepsilon) \leq 4 \left(\mathfrak {s} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta)\right) ^ {2}, \quad \forall \delta > 0. \tag {86}
$$

this immediately implies theorem 2.7 by taking  $\mathcal{z} = \mathcal{x}\times \mathcal{a}$ ,  $\mathcal{g} = \{(x,a)\mapsto |f(x,a) - f^{\star}(x,a)|\mid f\in \mathcal{f}\}$ , and  $\mathcal{p} = \mathcal{d}\otimes p$  for an arbitrary mapping  $p:\mathcal{x}\to \delta (\mathcal{a})$ . note that  $\mathcal{g}$  inherits the uniform glivenko-cantelli property from  $\mathcal{f}$  by the contraction principle.

the key step toward proving theorem e.1 is to prove an analogue of the result that holds whenever  $\mathcal{p}$  is the uniform distribution over a finite set of elements. for any sequence  $s = (z_{1},\ldots ,z_{n})$ , define

$$
\mathcal {g} _ {s} (\varepsilon) := \left\{g \in \mathcal {g}: \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} g ^ {2} (z _ {j}) \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \right\}.
$$

define  $w_{\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon)}(x) = \sup_{g\in \mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon)}g(x)$ . the finite-support analogue of theorem e.1 is as follows.

lemma e.1. for any sequence  $s = (z_{1},\ldots ,z_{n})$ , for any  $\zeta >0$ $\varepsilon >0$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \left\{w _ {\mathcal {g} _ {s} (\varepsilon)} (x _ {j}) > \zeta \right\} \leq \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{\zeta^ {2}} \check {\mathfrak {s}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \zeta) ^ {2} + \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{\zeta^ {2}} \check {\mathfrak {s}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \zeta) + \check {\mathfrak {s}} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \zeta) + 1 \\ \leq 4 \left(\frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{\zeta^ {2}} \vee 1\right) \cdot \mathfrak {s} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \zeta) ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

before proving this result, we show how it implies theorem e.1.

proof of theorem e.1. let  $1 > \delta > \varepsilon$  be fixed; the result is trivial for all other parameter values. we first appeal to the following lemma.

lemma e.2. for any  $\gamma \leq 1/4$ , there exists a finitely supported distribution  $\widehat{\mathcal{p}} = \mathrm{unif}((z_1, \ldots, z_n))$  such that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \big) \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\widehat {\mathcal {p}}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\widehat {\mathcal {p}}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} + \gamma \big) + \gamma .
$$

proof. let  $\gamma > 0$  be fixed. let  $\mathcal{p}_n$  denote the empirical distribution formed from  $n$  independent samples from  $\mathcal{p}$ . by hoeffding's inequality, we are guaranteed that for  $n$  sufficiently large, with probability at least  $1 - \gamma$ .

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \big) \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p} _ {n}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \big) + \gamma .
$$

next, we observe that since  $\mathcal{g}$  has the uniform glivenko-cantelli property, the class  $\left\{z \mapsto g^2(z) \mid g \in \mathcal{g}\right\}$  does as well (by the contraction principle, since  $|g| \leq 1$ ). this implies that for  $n$  sufficiently large,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\sup  _ {g \in \mathcal {g}} \left| \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p} _ {n}} ^ {2} - \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \right| > \gamma\right) \leq \gamma .
$$

if we take  $n$  large enough so that both claims hold and take a union bound, we are guaranteed that with probability at least  $1 - 2\gamma$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} \big) \leq \mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p} _ {n}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p} _ {n}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} + \gamma \big) + \gamma .
$$

since  $\gamma \leq 1 / 4$ , this event occurs with probability at least  $1 / 2$ . this establishes the existence of the distribution claimed in the lemma statement

write  $s = (z_{1},\ldots ,z_{n})$  , so that  $\widehat{\mathcal{p}} = \mathrm{unif}(s)$  . then we have

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\widehat {\mathcal {p}}} \big (\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\widehat {\mathcal {p}}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2} + \gamma \big) = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \big \{w _ {\mathcal {g} _ {s (\varepsilon^ {\prime})}} (z _ {i}) > \delta \big \},
$$

where  $\varepsilon^{\prime 2} \coloneqq n(\varepsilon^{2} + \gamma)$ . applying lemma e.1, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \{w _ {\mathcal {g} _ {s} (\varepsilon^ {\prime})} > \delta \} \leq \frac {4}{n} \left(\frac {\varepsilon^ {\prime 2}}{\delta^ {2}} \vee 1\right) \cdot (\mathfrak {s} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta)) ^ {2} \\ = \frac {4}{n} \frac {\varepsilon^ {\prime 2}}{\delta^ {2}} \cdot \left(\mathfrak {s} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta)\right) ^ {2} \\ = 4 \frac {\varepsilon^ {2} + \gamma}{\delta^ {2}} \cdot (\mathfrak {s} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta)) ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used that  $\varepsilon' \geq \delta$  by assumption. altogether, this implies that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p}} \left(\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \leq 4 \frac {\varepsilon^ {2} + \gamma}{\delta^ {2}} \cdot (\mathfrak {s} ^ {\text {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta)) ^ {2} + \gamma .
$$

since both sides are continuous functions of  $\gamma$  (in fact, the left-hand side does not depend on  $\gamma$  at all), we may take  $\gamma \to 0$  to conclude that

$$
\mathbb {p} _ {\mathcal {p}} \left(\exists g \in \mathcal {g}: g (z) > \delta , \| g \| _ {\mathcal {p}} ^ {2} \leq \varepsilon^ {2}\right) \leq 4 \frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{\delta^ {2}} \cdot (\mathfrak {s} ^ {\mathrm {v a l}} (\mathcal {g}, \delta)) ^ {2}.
$$

since this holds for all  $1 > \delta >\varepsilon$  , the result is established.

![](images/61bc320860f540adc8ec7ea5b65aff99fb0f0b9ee68719a773a4ec5746526564.jpg)

proof of lemma e.1. we begin with a definition.

definition e.1. consider a point  $z$  and a sequence  $a$  such that  $z \notin a$ . we say  $z$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on  $a$  with respect to  $\mathcal{g}$  if for all  $g \in \mathcal{g}$  such that  $\sum_{z' \in a} g^2(z') \leq \zeta^2$ , we have  $g(z) \leq \zeta$ . we say that  $z$  is  $\zeta$ -star-independent of  $a$  w.r.t.  $\mathcal{g}$  if  $z$  is not  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on  $a$ .

we first claim that for any  $i \in [n]$ , if  $w_{\mathcal{g}_s}(z_i) > \zeta$ , then  $z_i$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on at most  $\varepsilon^2 / \zeta^2$  disjoint subsequences of  $s$  (with respect to  $\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon)$ ). indeed, let  $g$  be a function in  $\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon)$  such that  $g(z_i) > \zeta$ . if  $z_i$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on a particular subsequence  $(z_{i_1}, \ldots, z_{i_k}) \subset s$  but  $g(z_i) > \zeta$ , we must have

$$
\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k} g ^ {2} \left(z _ {i _ {j}}\right) > \zeta^ {2}.
$$

if there are  $n$  such disjoint sequences, we have

$$
n \zeta^ {2} <   \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} g ^ {2} (z _ {j}) \leq \varepsilon^ {2},
$$

so  $n < \varepsilon^2 /\zeta^2$

now we claim that for any sequence  $(z_{1},\ldots ,z_{\tau})$ , there is some  $j\in [\tau ]$  such that  $z_{j}$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on at least  $\lfloor (\tau -1) / d\rfloor /(d + 1)$  disjoint subsequences of  $(z_{1},\dots ,z_{\tau})$  (with respect to  $\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon)$ ), where  $d\equiv \check{\mathfrak{s}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon),\zeta)$ . this is a straightforward corollary of the following lemma, which is purely combinatorial.

lemma e.3. let  $a$  be a finite set with  $\tau$  elements, and let  $d < \tau$  be a positive integer. consider any function  $\text{dependent} : 2^a \times a \to \{\text{true}, \text{false}\}$ , and let us say that  $x$  is dependent on  $a' \subseteq a$  if  $\text{dependent}(a', x) = \text{true}$ . suppose  $\text{dependent}$  has the property that for every subset  $a' \subseteq a$  with  $|a'| > d$ , there exists  $x \in a'$  such that  $x$  is dependent on  $a' \setminus \{x\}$  (i.e.,  $\text{dependent}(a' \setminus \{x\}, x) = \text{true}$ ). then there must exist an element of  $a$  that is dependent on at least  $\lfloor (\tau - 1)/d \rfloor/(d + 1)$  disjoint subsets of  $a$ .

let  $(z_{t_1},\dots ,z_{t_\tau})$  consist of all elements of  $(z_{1},\ldots ,z_{n})$  for which  $w_{\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon)}(z) > \zeta$ . each element of  $(z_{t_1},\dots ,z_{t_\tau})$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on at most  $\varepsilon^2 /\zeta^2$  disjoint subsets of  $(z_{t_1},\dots ,z_{t_\tau})$ , and we claim that by lemma e.3, one element is dependent on at least  $\lfloor (\tau -1) / d\rfloor /(d + 1)$  disjoint subsets. this implies that  $\lfloor (\tau -1) / d\rfloor /(d + 1)\leq \varepsilon^2 /\zeta^2$ , so that  $\tau \leq (\varepsilon^{2} / \zeta^{2})(d^{2} + d) + d + 1$ .

let us carefully verify that we can indeed apply lemma e.3 here. take  $a = \{1, \dots, \tau\}$  to be the index set of  $(z_{t_1}, \ldots, z_{t_\tau})$ , and define  $\text{dependent}(a', i) = \text{true}$  if  $z_{t_i}$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on  $(z_{i_k})_{k \in a'}$ . with  $d = \check{\mathfrak{s}}^{\text{val}}(\mathcal{g}_s(\varepsilon), \zeta)$ , any sequence of more than  $d$  (potentially non-unique) elements of  $\mathcal{z}$  cannot witness the value function star number, so for any  $a' \subseteq a$  with  $|a'| \geq d$ , there must at least one  $i \in a'$  such that for all  $g \in \mathcal{g}(\varepsilon)$ ,  $\sum_{k \in a' \setminus \{i\}} g^2(z_{t_k}) \leq \zeta^2$  implies that  $g(z_{t_i}) \leq \zeta$ . such a  $z_{t_i}$  is  $\zeta$ -star-dependent on  $(z_{t_k})_{k \in a' \setminus \{i\}}$ , so dependent satisfies the condition of the lemma. since subsequences of  $(z_{t_1}, \ldots, z_{t_\tau})$  are in one-to-one correspondence with subsets of  $a$ , lemma e.3 grants the desired result.

proof of lemma e.3. we provide two different proofs: one based on the probabilistic method, and one based on a direct counting argument. the first proof leads to slightly worse constants.

probabilistic proof. consider  $a = \{1, \dots, \tau\}$ . suppose we sample  $a' \subseteq a$  with  $|a'| = d + 1$  uniformly at random. then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} 1 \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \max  _ {x \in a ^ {\prime}} \mathbb {i} \left\{\text {d e p e n d e n t} \left(a ^ {\prime} \backslash \{x \}, x\right) = \text {t r u e} \right\} \right] \\ \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {x} \mathbb {i} \{x \in a ^ {\prime}, \text {d e p e n d e n t} \left(a ^ {\prime} \backslash \{x \}, x\right) = \text {t r u e} \} \right] \\ = \sum_ {x} \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbb {i} \left\{x \in a ^ {\prime}, \text {d e p e n d e n t} \left(a ^ {\prime} \backslash \{x \}, x\right) = \text {t r u e} \right\} \right] \\ = \sum_ {x} \mathbb {p} (x \in a ^ {\prime}) \mathbb {p} (\text {d e p e n d e n t} (a ^ {\prime} \backslash \{x \}, x) = \text {t r u e} \mid x \in a ^ {\prime}). \\ \end{array}
$$

observe that  $\mathbb{p}(x\in a^{\prime})\leq \frac{d + 1}{\tau - d}$ . now, consider a set  $a''$  with  $|a''| = d$  chosen from  $a\setminus \{x\}$  uniformly at random. then we have  $\mathbb{p}(\text{dependent}(a'\setminus \{x\},x) = \text{true} \mid x\in a') = \mathbb{p}(\text{dependent}(a'',x) = \text{true})$ . this allows us to upper bound by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \sum_ {x} \frac {d + 1}{\tau - d} \mathbb {p} (\text {d e p e n d e n t} (a ^ {\prime \prime}, x) = \text {t r u e}) \\ \leq (d + 1) \frac {\tau}{\tau - d} \cdot \max  _ {x} \mathbb {p} (\text {d e p e n d e n t} (a ^ {\prime \prime}, x) = \text {t r u e}). \\ \end{array}
$$

it follows that there exists  $x$  such that  $\mathbb{p}(\text{dependent}(a'', x) = \text{true}) \geq (1 - d / \tau) \frac{1}{d + 1}$ . now, let  $n = \left\lfloor \frac{\tau - 1}{d} \right\rfloor$ , and let  $x_1, \ldots, x_n$  be a collection of disjoint subsets of  $a \setminus \{x\}$  with  $|x_i| = d$ , sampled uniformly at random. then by symmetry, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \{\text {d e p e n d e n t} (x _ {i}, x) = \text {t r u e} \} \right] = n \cdot \mathbb {p} (\text {d e p e n d e n t} (a ^ {\prime \prime}, x) = \text {t r u e}) \\ \geq \left(1 - \frac {d}{\tau}\right) \frac {n}{d + 1} = \left(1 - \frac {d}{\tau}\right) \frac {1}{d + 1} \left\lfloor \frac {\tau - 1}{d} \right\rfloor . \\ \end{array}
$$

we conclude that there exists some  $x \in a$  and a collection  $\{x_i\}$  of at least  $\left(1 - \frac{d}{\tau}\right)\frac{1}{d + 1}\left\lfloor \frac{\tau - 1}{d}\right\rfloor$  disjoint subsets of  $a \setminus \{x\}$  such that  $\text{dependent}(x_i, x) = \text{true}$  for all  $i$ .

combinatorial proof. this is a constructive proof based on a counting argument, which enables us to directly find an element in  $a$  that is dependent on at least  $\lfloor (\tau - 1) / d \rfloor / (d + 1)$  disjoint subsets of  $a$ .

to simplify notation, let us assign the elements of  $a$  an arbitrary order and represent  $a$  as  $\{1,\ldots ,\tau \}$ . for any  $(d + 1)$ -size subset  $a^{\prime}$  of  $a$ , by the property of dependent, we know that  $\exists x\in a^{\prime}$  such that  $x$  is dependent on  $a^{\prime}\backslash \{x\}$ , and we define

$$
\mu \left(a ^ {\prime}\right) := \min  \{x \in a ^ {\prime}: \text {d e p e n d e n t} \left(a ^ {\prime} \backslash \{x \}, x\right) = \text {t r u e} \}.
$$

note that  $\mu (a^{\prime})$  is always well-defined as long as  $|a^{\prime}|\geq d + 1$

let  $n > 0$  and  $c \in \{0, \dots, d - 1\}$  be integers such that  $\tau - 1 = nd + c$ . we define a  $(1, n \times d, c)$ -partition of  $a$  as a set-valued sequence

$$
\left(x _ {0}, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}, x _ {n + 1}\right)
$$

with the property that:

1.  $x_0,\ldots ,x_{n + 1}$  are disjoint.  
2.  $\bigcup_{n = 0}^{n + 1}x_n = a.$  
3.  $|x_0| = 1$  
4.  $|x_1| = \dots = |x_n| = d$ .  
5.  $|x_{n + 1}| = c$

let  $\operatorname{par}(a;d)$  denote the set of all possible  $(1,n\times d,c)$ -partitions of  $a$ . note that in particular that different permutations of  $(x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})$  may yield different  $(1,n\times d,c)$ -partitions.

now let us consider an optimal  $(1, n \times d, c)$ -partition of  $a$  such that  $\sum_{n=1}^{n} \mathbb{i}\{\mu(x_0 \cup x_n) \in x_0\}$  is maximized. let  $(x_0^\star, x_1^\star, \ldots, x_n^\star, x_{n+1}^\star)$  denote this partition (if there are multiple optimal partitions, then we just pick one of them). by the optimality of  $(x_0^\star, x_1^\star, \ldots, x_n^\star, x_{n+1}^\star)$ , we have

$$
\sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \left\{\mu \left(x _ {0} ^ {\star} \cup x _ {n} ^ {\star}\right) \in x _ {0} ^ {\star} \right\} \geq \frac {1}{| \operatorname {p a r} (a ; d) |} \sum_ {\left(x _ {0}, \dots , x _ {n + 1}\right) \in \operatorname {p a r} (a; d)} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \left\{\mu \left(x _ {0} \cup x _ {n}\right) \in x _ {0} \right\}, \tag {87}
$$

and we use  $\lambda$  to denote the right-hand side of (87). consider the single element in  $x_0^\star$ , denoted as  $x^\star$ . since  $x_1^\star, \ldots, x_n^\star$  are disjoint and

$$
\sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \left\{\text {d e p e n d e n t} \left(x _ {n}, x ^ {\star}\right) = \text {t r u e} \right\} \geq \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \left\{\mu \left(x _ {0} ^ {\star} \cup x _ {n} ^ {\star}\right) \in x _ {0} ^ {\star} \right\} \geq \lambda ,
$$

we know that  $x^{\star}$  is dependent on at least  $[\lambda]$  disjoint subsets of  $a$ .

in what follows, we calculate the value of  $\lambda$ .

step 1. we calculate the value of  $|\mathrm{par}(a;d)|$  using the following identity:

$$
\left| \operatorname {p a r} (a; d) \right| = \binom {\tau} {1} \cdot \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 1} \binom {\tau - 1 - n d} {d} = \tau \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 1} \binom {(n - n) d + c} {d}. \tag {88}
$$

this holds by a direct counting argument: there are  $\binom{\tau}{1} = \tau$  choices for  $x_0$ ,  $\binom{\tau-1-d}{d}$  choices for  $x_1$  given each such choice,  $\binom{\tau-1-2d}{d}$  choices for  $x_2$  given the preceding two choices, all the way on to  $\binom{\tau-1-(n-1)d}{d}$  choices for  $x_n$ ; the remaining elements must be assigned to  $x_{n+1}$ .

# step 2. we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {\left(x _ {0}, \dots , x _ {n + 1}\right) \in \operatorname {p} _ {\operatorname {a r}} (a; d)} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {i} \left\{\mu \left(x _ {0} \cup x _ {n}\right) \in x _ {0} \right\} \\ = \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {\left(x _ {0}, \dots , x _ {n + 1}\right) \in \operatorname {p a r} (a; d)} \mathbb {i} \left\{\mu \left(x _ {0} \cup x _ {n}\right) \in x _ {0} \right\} \\ \stackrel {(i)} {=} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {x \in a} \sum_ {x _ {n} \subset a: | x _ {n} | = d, x \notin x _ {n}} \sum_ {\substack {x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n - 1}, x _ {n + 1}, \ldots , x _ {n + 1} \\ \text{s.t.} (\{x \}, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n - 1}, x _ {n}, x _ {n + 1}, \ldots , x _ {n + 1}) \in \operatorname {p a r} (a; d)}} \mathbb {i} \{\mu (\{x \} \cup x _ {n}) = x \} \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {=} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {n} \sum_ {a ^ {\prime} \subset a: | a ^ {\prime} | = d + 1} \sum_ {x \in a ^ {\prime}} \sum_ { \begin{array}{c} x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n - 1}, x _ {n + 1}, \ldots , x _ {n + 1} \\ \text {s . t .} (\{x \}, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n - 1}, a ^ {\prime} \setminus \{x \}, x _ {n + 1}, \ldots , x _ {n + 1}) \in \operatorname {p a r} (a; d) \end{array} } \mathbb {i} \{\mu (a ^ {\prime}) = x \} \\ \stackrel{(iii)}{=}\sum_{n = 1}^{n}\sum_{a^{\prime}\subset a:|a^{\prime}| = d + 1}\sum_{\substack{x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n - 1},x_{n + 1},\ldots ,x_{n + 1}\\ \text{s.t.} (\{\mu (a^{\prime})\} ,x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n - 1},a^{\prime}\setminus \{\mu (a^{\prime})\} ,x_{n + 1},\ldots ,x_{n + 1})\in \operatorname {par}(a;d)}}1 \\ \stackrel {(i v)} {=} n \cdot \left( \begin{array}{c} \tau \\ d + 1 \end{array} \right) \cdot \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 2} \binom {\tau - d - 1 - n d} {d} \\ = n \cdot \binom {n d + c + 1} {d + 1} \cdot \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 2} \binom {(n - n - 1) d + c} {d}. \tag {89} \\ \end{array}
$$

here  $(i)$  rewrites the sum over partitions to make the choices for  $x_0$  and  $x_{n}$  explicit,  $(ii)$  rewrites this once more by considering the choice of the  $x_{n}$  and  $x_0$  as equivalent to the choice of a set  $a^\prime$  with  $|a^{\prime}| = d + 1$  and an element  $x\in a^{\prime}$  (so that  $x_0 = \{x\}$  and  $x_{n} = a^{\prime}\setminus \{x\}$ ), and  $(iii)$  restricts only to elements for which  $\mu (a^{\prime}) = x$ . they key step above is  $(iv)$ , which can be seen to hold as follows. for each choice of  $n$  in the outermost sum in line  $(iv)$ :

- there are  $\binom{\tau}{d+1}$  choices for  $a'$  in the middle sum.  
- for each choice of  $n$  and  $a'$ , the only constraint on  $x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n - 1},x_{n + 1},\ldots ,x_{n + 1}$  is that they form a partition of  $a\setminus a^{\prime}$ . there are  $\binom{\tau-(d+1)}{d}$  choices for  $x_{1}$ ,  $\binom{\tau-(d+1)-d}{d}$  choices for  $x_{2}$  given each such choice, and eventually  $\binom{\tau-(d+1)-(n-2)d}{d}$  choices for  $x_{n}$  given all the preceding choices; all elements left over from  $x_{0},\ldots ,x_{n}$  are assigned to  $x_{n + 1}$ .

the final equality above simply substitutes in  $\tau = nd + c + 1$ .

step 3. combining (88) and (89), we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda = \frac{n\cdot\binom{nd + c + 1}{d + 1}\cdot\prod_{n = 0}^{n - 2}\binom{(n - n - 1)d + c}{d}}{\operatorname{par}(a;d)|} \\ = \frac {n \cdot \binom {n d + c + 1} {d + 1} \cdot \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 2} \binom {(n - n - 1) d + c} {d}}{\tau \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 1} \binom {(n - n) d + c} {d}} \\ = \frac {n \cdot \binom {n d + c + 1} {d + 1} \cdot \prod_ {n = 1} ^ {n - 1} \binom {(n - n) d + c} {d}}{\tau \prod_ {n = 0} ^ {n - 1} \binom {(n - n) d + c} {d}} \\ = \frac {n \cdot \binom {n d + c + 1} {d + 1}}{\tau \cdot \binom {n d + c} {d}} = \frac {n (n d + c + 1)}{\tau (d + 1)} = \frac {n}{d + 1} = \frac {\lfloor \frac {\tau - 1}{d} \rfloor}{d + 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this implies that  $x^{\star} \in a$  is dependent on at least  $\lfloor (\tau - 1) / d \rfloor / (d + 1)$  disjoint subsets of  $a$ .

# e.3 proofs for eluder dimension results

proof of proposition 2.3. let  $d \in \mathbb{n}$  and  $\delta \in (0,1)$  be fixed. let  $\mathcal{x} = [d]$  and  $\mathcal{a} = \{0,1\}$ . set  $f^{\star}(x,0) = 0$  and  $f^{\star}(x,1) = \delta$  for all  $x$ . for each  $i$ , define a function  $f_{i}$  as follows.

$f_{i}(x,1) = \delta$  for all  $x$  
$f_{i}(j,0) = 0$  for all  $j <   i$  
$f_{i}(j,0) = \delta$  for all  $j\geq i$

let  $\mathcal{f} = \{f^{\star}, f_1, \ldots, f_d\}$ . we have  $\mathfrak{e}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f}, \delta/2) \geq d$  by taking  $(1,0), \ldots, (d,0)$  and  $f_1, \ldots, f_d$  as witnesses, since for each  $i$ ,  $|f_i(i,0) - f^{\star}(i,0)| = \delta > \delta/2$  and

$$
\sum_ {j <   i} (f _ {i} (j, 0) - f ^ {\star} (j, 0)) ^ {2} = 0.
$$

we now upper bound the value function star number. clearly  $\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta^{\prime}) = 0$  for any  $\delta^\prime \geq \delta$ , so consider a fixed scale parameter  $\delta^{\prime} < \delta$ . suppose we have a set of points  $(i_1,0),\ldots ,(i_m,0)$  and functions  $f_{j_1},\dots ,f_{j_m}$  that witness  $\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{f^{\star}}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta^{\prime})$ , with  $i_1 < i_2 < \dots ,i_m$  (we must have 0 as the action for each witness, since all functions agree on the value for action 1). since  $|f_{j_1}(i_1,0) - f^{\star}(i_1,0)| > \delta '$ , we must have  $j_{m}\leq i_{m}$ . but on the other hand, we have

$$
\sum_ {l > 1} ^ {m} \left(f _ {j _ {1}} \left(i _ {l}, 0\right) - f ^ {\star} \left(i _ {l}, 0\right)\right) ^ {2} = \delta^ {2} (m - 1),
$$

since  $f_{j_1}(i_l,0) = \delta$  for all  $l > 2$ . since we need  $\delta^2 (m - 1)\leq (\delta ')^2$ , we must have  $m\leq 2$ , so we conclude that  $\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{f^*}^{\mathrm{val}}(\mathcal{f},\delta ')\leq 2$  for all  $\delta^{\prime} < \delta$ .
